2022-2024 年,加强肯尼亚西部五岁以下儿童常规卫生服务的追赶 疫苗接种 服务。

Strengthening catch-up vaccination delivery in routine health services for children under five in Western Kenya, 2022-2024.
作者： Pierre Muhoza, Cyrus Kagecha, Kirsten F. Ward, Hong-Jiang Gao, Dominic Ouma, Mohamed Adan, Ledor S. Igboh, Youngjoo Park, Anh N. Ly, Damaris Jepkosgei
摘要中文翻译背景:追赶 疫苗接种 可以缩小人群免疫差距,但在低收入和中等收入国家 (LMIC),融入常规免疫服务仍然有限。根据肯尼亚的 2023 年 疫苗接种 追赶政策,我们评估了一项多策略干预措施,以加强西亚县常规卫生服务中的系统识别、资格筛查和漏服剂量的提供。方法:在 12 个月的时间里,在通过分层随机抽样选出的 55 个公共卫生机构中实施了五项策略,涉及医疗保健工作者 (HCW) 能力、疫苗接种 文档、违约者跟踪、免疫筛查和护理人员参与。使用前后设计,我们评估了免疫接种率、疫苗接种 (MOV) 错过的机会以及系统准备情况。基线(2022 年 11 月至 2023 年 1 月)和终点(2024 年 10 月至 12 月)评估包括设施评估、医护人员访谈、对年龄 < 36 个月的儿童进行的护理人员退出调查(HBR;n = 658)以及设施免疫登记摘要(n = 1650 名 12-23 个月的儿童)。分析考虑了抽样设计、聚类,并使用了 HBR 数据的广义估计方程。结果:出于任何原因访问设施的儿童中 MOV 患病率 从 94.4% 下降至 20.9% (p < 0.001)。12-23 个月龄儿童的完全免疫率从 44.9% 增加到 63.6% (p < 0.001),各种抗原的吸收率有所提高。麻疹-风疹第一剂吸收率从 51.5% 增加到 78.0%,第二剂吸收率从 18.8% 增加到 36.9%(两者的 p < 0.001)。设施的资格筛查从 60.6% 扩大到 86.8% (p = 0.012),违约者跟踪从 48.5% 提高到 91.6% (p < 0.001),医护人员的知识和信心显着增加。结论:多策略干预与较少的 MOV 和提高的吸收率相关,这表明将追赶型 疫苗接种 纳入常规卫生服务可以加强分散的中低收入国家的免疫系统。肯尼亚的追赶型 疫苗接种 政策为将追赶型 疫苗接种 引入常规卫生服务提供了框架。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
根据肯尼亚的 2023 年 疫苗接种 追赶政策,我们评估了一项多策略干预措施,以加强西亚县常规卫生服务中的系统识别、资格筛查和漏服剂量的提供。
证据基础与方法
基线(2022 年 11 月至 2023 年 1 月)和终点(2024 年 10 月至 12 月)评估包括设施评估、医护人员访谈、对年龄 < 36 个月的儿童进行的护理人员退出调查(HBR;n = 658)以及设施免疫登记摘要(n = 1650 名 12-23 个月的儿童)。
共识与结论
12-23 个月龄儿童的完全免疫率从 44.9% 增加到 63.6% (p < 0.001),各种抗原的吸收率有所提高。
争议与缺口
追赶 疫苗接种 可以缩小人群免疫差距,但在低收入和中等收入国家 (LMIC),融入常规免疫服务仍然有限。
科研与实践启示
多策略干预与较少的 MOV 和提高的吸收率相关,这表明将追赶型 疫苗接种 纳入常规卫生服务可以加强分散的中低收入国家的免疫系统。

Strengthening catch-up vaccination delivery in routine health services for children under five in Western Kenya, 2022-2024.

Authors: Pierre Muhoza, Cyrus Kagecha, Kirsten F. Ward, Hong-Jiang Gao, Dominic Ouma, Mohamed Adan, Ledor S. Igboh, Youngjoo Park, Anh N. Ly, Damaris Jepkosgei
Original abstract (English)
BACKGROUND: Catch-up vaccination can close population immunity gaps, yet integration into routine immunization services remains limited in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs). Following Kenya's 2023 catch-up vaccination policy, we evaluated a multi-strategy intervention to strengthen systematic identification, eligibility screening, and delivery of missed doses within routine health services in Siaya County. METHODS: Over 12 months, five strategies addressing healthcare worker (HCW) capacity, vaccination documentation, defaulter tracking, immunization screening, and caregiver engagement were implemented in 55 public health facilities selected through stratified random sampling. Using a pre-post design, we assessed immunization uptake, missed opportunities for vaccination (MOVs), and system readiness. Baseline (November 2022-January 2023) and endline (October-December 2024) evaluations included facility assessments, HCW interviews, caregiver exit surveys among children aged <36 months with home-based records (HBRs; n = 658), and facility immunization register abstractions (n = 1650 children aged 12-23 months). Analyses accounted for sampling design, clustering, and used generalized estimating equations for HBR data. RESULTS: MOV prevalence among children visiting facilities for any reason declined from 94.4% to 20.9% (p < 0.001). Full immunization among children aged 12-23 months increased from 44.9% to 63.6% (p < 0.001), with uptake improving across antigens. Measles-rubella first-dose uptake increased from 51.5% to 78.0%, and second-dose uptake from 18.8% to 36.9% (p < 0.001 for both). Eligibility screening expanded from 60.6% to 86.8% of facilities (p = 0.012), defaulter tracking improved from 48.5% to 91.6% (p < 0.001), and HCW knowledge and confidence increased significantly. CONCLUSION: The multi-strategy intervention was associated with fewer MOVs and improved uptake, suggesting that embedding catch-up vaccination within routine health services can strengthen immunization systems in decentralized LMIC contexts. Kenya's catch-up vaccination policy provided a framework for introducing catch-up vaccination into routine health services.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Following Kenya's 2023 catch-up vaccination policy, we evaluated a multi-strategy intervention to strengthen systematic identification, eligibility screening, and delivery of missed doses within routine health services in Siaya County.
Evidence base and method
Baseline (November 2022-January 2023) and endline (October-December 2024) evaluations included facility assessments, HCW interviews, caregiver exit surveys among children aged <36 months with home-based records (HBRs; n = 658), and facility immunization register abstractions (n = 1650 children aged 12-23 months).
Consensus and conclusions
Full immunization among children aged 12-23 months increased from 44.9% to 63.6% (p < 0.001), with uptake improving across antigens.
Controversies and gaps
Catch-up vaccination can close population immunity gaps, yet integration into routine immunization services remains limited in low- and middle-income countries (LMICs).
Research and practice implications
The multi-strategy intervention was associated with fewer MOVs and improved uptake, suggesting that embedding catch-up vaccination within routine health services can strengthen immunization systems in decentralized LMIC contexts.

用于透皮给药的微阵列贴片:对技术创新、CMC 挑战、临床转化和全球健康实施的批判性叙述回顾

Microarray patches for transdermal drug delivery: a critical narrative review of technological innovations, CMC challenges, clinical translation, and global health implementation
作者： Priyabrata Pattnaik, Lawrence Sena Tuglo, Mohammed Alissa, Snehasish Mishra, Abdullah Alghamdi, Mohammed A. Alshehri, Tawfiq N. Juraybi, Helal G. Alanazi, Ghada M. Alnafesah, Ranjan K. Mohapatra
摘要中文翻译微阵列贴片 (MAP),也称为微针贴片,是药剂和 疫苗 给药的范式转变。通过利用微米级的突出物突破角质层,MAP 弥补了透皮贴剂的便利性和皮下注射的功效之间的差距。这篇重要的叙述性综述综合了透皮给药 MAP 技术的进展,重点关注技术创新、化学、制造和控制 (CMC) 挑战、临床转化、监管考虑和全球健康实施。特别强调 CMC 挑战,包括灭菌和稳定性分析,以及临床试验的情况,强调 麻疹 和风疹关键 III 期研究的最新框架。最后,我们评估了 MAP 对低收入和中等收入国家 (LMIC) 的规划适用性,解决了冷链物流和锥虫恐惧症等关键障碍。从逐个产品模型到平台工业化模型的协调一致的 进化 是必要的,涉及 疫苗、聚合物和设备生产公司、监管机构、包括世界卫生组织 (WHO) 在内的全球卫生机构和技术推动者。麻疹-Rubella 程序提供了一个重要的模板。MAP 的价值不在于更换注射器,而是实现 疫苗接种 的公平分配;减少 疫苗接种 对基础设施依赖的策略。随着科学创新、升级、统一监管框架和公平获取的进步,MAP 将实现全球相关性。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
微阵列贴片 (MAP) 是药剂和 疫苗 管理的范式转变,重点关注技术创新、化学、制造和控制 (CMC) 挑战、临床转化、监管考虑和全球健康实施。该综述综合了透皮给药 MAP 技术的进展,特别强调了 CMC 挑战和临床试验前景,特别是在 麻疹 和风疹方面。
证据基础与方法
这篇重要的叙述性综述综合了透皮给药 MAP 技术的进展,重点关注技术创新、化学、制造和控制 (CMC) 挑战、临床转化、监管考虑和全球健康实施。特别强调 CMC 挑战,包括灭菌和稳定性分析,以及临床试验的情况,强调 麻疹 和风疹关键 III 期研究的最新框架。
共识与结论
审查最后评估了 MAP 对低收入和中等收入国家 (LMIC) 的规划适用性,解决了冷链物流和锥虫恐惧症等关键障碍。
争议与缺口
该审查确定了 CMC 挑战以及从逐个产品模型到平台工业化模型的协调 进化 的需要,涉及 疫苗、聚合物和设备生产公司、监管机构、包括世界卫生组织 (WHO) 在内的全球卫生机构以及技术推动者,这是关键差距。
科研与实践启示
MAP 的价值不在于更换注射器,而是实现 疫苗接种 的公平分配;减少 疫苗接种 对基础设施依赖的策略。

Microarray patches for transdermal drug delivery: a critical narrative review of technological innovations, CMC challenges, clinical translation, and global health implementation

Authors: Priyabrata Pattnaik, Lawrence Sena Tuglo, Mohammed Alissa, Snehasish Mishra, Abdullah Alghamdi, Mohammed A. Alshehri, Tawfiq N. Juraybi, Helal G. Alanazi, Ghada M. Alnafesah, Ranjan K. Mohapatra
Original abstract (English)
Microarray patches (MAPs), alternatively known as microneedle patches, is a paradigm shift in the administration of pharmaceutical agents and vaccines. By utilising micron-scale projections to breach the stratum corneum, MAPs bridge the gap between the convenience of transdermal patches and the efficacy of hypodermic injections. This critical narrative review synthesises advances in MAP technology for transdermal drug delivery, focusing on technological innovations, chemistry, manufacturing, and control (CMC) challenges, clinical translation, regulatory considerations, and global health implementation. Special emphasis is placed on CMC challenges, including sterilisation and stability profiling, as well as the landscape of Clinical Trials, highlighting recent frameworks for pivotal Phase III studies in measles and rubella. We conclude by evaluating the programmatic suitability of MAPs for low- and middle-income countries (LMICs), addressing critical barriers such as cold-chain logistics and trypanophobia. A coordinated evolution from product-by-product model to platform-industrialisation model involving vaccine, polymer and device producing companies, regulators, global-health agencies including World health organization (WHO), and technology enablers is necessary. The Measles-Rubella programme provides an important template. MAPs’ value is not about replacing a syringe but enabling equitable distribution of vaccination; a strategy to make vaccination less dependent on infrastructure. As scientific innovations, upscaling, unified regulatory frameworks and equitable access advance, MAPs will achieve global relevance.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Microarray patches (MAPs) are a paradigm shift in the administration of pharmaceutical agents and vaccines, focusing on technological innovations, chemistry, manufacturing, and control (CMC) challenges, clinical translation, regulatory considerations, and global health implementation. The review synthesises advances in MAP technology for transdermal drug delivery, with special emphasis on CMC challenges and the landscape of Clinical Trials, particularly in measles and rubella.
Evidence base and method
This critical narrative review synthesises advances in MAP technology for transdermal drug delivery, focusing on technological innovations, chemistry, manufacturing, and control (CMC) challenges, clinical translation, regulatory considerations, and global health implementation. Special emphasis is placed on CMC challenges, including sterilisation and stability profiling, as well as the landscape of Clinical Trials, highlighting recent frameworks for pivotal Phase III studies in measles and rubella.
Consensus and conclusions
The review concludes by evaluating the programmatic suitability of MAPs for low- and middle-income countries (LMICs), addressing critical barriers such as cold-chain logistics and trypanophobia.
Controversies and gaps
The review identifies CMC challenges and the need for a coordinated evolution from product-by-product model to platform-industrialisation model involving vaccine, polymer and device producing companies, regulators, global-health agencies including World health organization (WHO), and technology enablers as critical gaps.
Research and practice implications
MAPs’ value is not about replacing a syringe but enabling equitable distribution of vaccination; a strategy to make vaccination less dependent on infrastructure.

溶瘤病毒治疗的进展:卵巢癌的新视野

Advancements in oncolytic virus therapy: a new horizon for ovarian cancer
作者： Qiu-Man Wang, Yu Sun, Ru-Xin Liu, Bei-Hua Kong, Jie Li, Kun Song, Hui Zhang, Cunzhong Yuan
摘要中文翻译卵巢癌仍然是最致命的妇科恶性肿瘤,标准 治疗 有限的耐久性促进了新治疗策略的开发。溶瘤病毒选择性地在肿瘤细胞内复制并裂解肿瘤细胞,释放肿瘤抗原和炎症信号,从而将免疫抑制的肿瘤微环境转变为免疫炎症状态。本综述评估了卵巢癌中使用的主要平台,包括腺病毒、呼肠孤病毒、麻疹病毒、牛痘病毒、单纯疱疹病毒和水泡性口炎病毒。我们比较了增强肿瘤选择性复制、细胞进入、转基因递送和局部免疫激活的工程方法,并讨论了克服中和抗体、有限的瘤内扩散、脱靶毒性和肿瘤异质性的策略。特别强调细胞因子或双特异性 T 细胞接合病毒及其与化疗、免疫检查点阻断和细胞免疫疗法的组合。早期研究支持腹腔内给药的可行性,而 Olvi-Vec 的单臂 2 期研究随后进行铂类化疗报告了令人鼓舞的结果,并且随机 3 期试验正在进行中。尚无溶瘤病毒被批准用于治疗卵巢癌;然而,目前的证据支持生物标志物指导和合理排序的临床开发。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
卵巢癌仍然是最致命的妇科恶性肿瘤,标准 治疗 有限的耐久性促进了新治疗策略的开发。本综述评估了用于卵巢癌的主要平台,包括 麻疹病毒,并讨论了它们与其他疗法结合的潜力。
证据基础与方法
该综述是对卵巢癌溶瘤病毒平台(包括 麻疹病毒)的系统分析。它评估增强肿瘤选择性复制、细胞进入、转基因传递和局部免疫激活的工程方法。该综述还讨论了克服中和抗体、有限的瘤内扩散、脱靶毒性和肿瘤异质性的策略。
共识与结论
早期研究支持腹腔内递送溶瘤病毒的可行性,Olvi-Vec 随后进行铂类化疗的单臂 2 期研究报告了令人鼓舞的结果。一项随机 3 期试验正在进行中,表明进一步评估基于 麻疹病毒 的卵巢癌疗法的潜力。
争议与缺口
该综述强调了中和抗体、有限的瘤内扩散、脱靶毒性和肿瘤异质性等挑战,这些挑战是溶瘤病毒有效性的障碍。这些问题仍未得到解决,需要进一步调查。
科研与实践启示
特别强调细胞因子或双特异性 T 细胞接合病毒及其与化疗、免疫检查点阻断和细胞免疫疗法的组合。该综述表明,生物标志物引导和合理排序的临床开发对于推进基于 麻疹病毒 的卵巢癌治疗是必要的。

Advancements in oncolytic virus therapy: a new horizon for ovarian cancer

Authors: Qiu-Man Wang, Yu Sun, Ru-Xin Liu, Bei-Hua Kong, Jie Li, Kun Song, Hui Zhang, Cunzhong Yuan
Original abstract (English)
Ovarian cancer remains the most lethal gynecological malignancy, and the limited durability of standard treatments has motivated the development of new therapeutic strategies. Oncolytic viruses selectively replicate within and lyse tumor cells, releasing tumor antigens and inflammatory signals that can convert an immunosuppressive tumor microenvironment into an immune-inflamed state. This review evaluates the principal platforms used in ovarian cancer, including adenovirus, reovirus, measles virus, vaccinia virus, herpes simplex virus, and vesicular stomatitis virus. We compare engineering approaches that enhance tumor-selective replication, cellular entry, transgene delivery, and local immune activation, and discuss strategies to overcome neutralizing antibodies, restricted intratumoral spread, off-target toxicity, and tumor heterogeneity. Particular emphasis is placed on cytokine- or bispecific T-cell engager-armed viruses and their combination with chemotherapy, immune checkpoint blockade, and cellular immunotherapy. Early-phase studies support the feasibility of intraperitoneal delivery, whereas a single-arm phase 2 study of Olvi-Vec followed by platinum-based chemotherapy reported encouraging results, and a randomized phase 3 trial is ongoing. No oncolytic viruses have been approved for ovarian cancer; however, current evidence supports biomarker-guided and rationally sequenced clinical development.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
Ovarian cancer remains the most lethal gynecological malignancy, and the limited durability of standard treatments has motivated the development of new therapeutic strategies. This review evaluates the principal platforms used in ovarian cancer, including measles virus, and discusses their potential in combination with other therapies.
Evidence base and method
The review is a systematic analysis of oncolytic virus platforms, including measles virus, for ovarian cancer. It evaluates engineering approaches that enhance tumor-selective replication, cellular entry, transgene delivery, and local immune activation. The review also discusses strategies to overcome neutralizing antibodies, restricted intratumoral spread, off-target toxicity, and tumor heterogeneity.
Consensus and conclusions
Early-phase studies support the feasibility of intraperitoneal delivery of oncolytic viruses, and a single-arm phase 2 study of Olvi-Vec followed by platinum-based chemotherapy reported encouraging results. A randomized phase 3 trial is ongoing, indicating potential for further evaluation of measles virus-based therapies in ovarian cancer.
Controversies and gaps
The review highlights the challenges of neutralizing antibodies, restricted intratumoral spread, off-target toxicity, and tumor heterogeneity as barriers to the effectiveness of oncolytic viruses. These issues remain unresolved and require further investigation.
Research and practice implications
Particular emphasis is placed on cytokine- or bispecific T-cell engager-armed viruses and their combination with chemotherapy, immune checkpoint blockade, and cellular immunotherapy. The review suggests that biomarker-guided and rationally sequenced clinical development is necessary for advancing measles virus-based therapies in ovarian cancer.

旅行者和人畜病原体 传播:人畜共患病和反向人畜共患疾病的一次健康回顾

Travelers and Human–Animal Pathogen Transmission: A One Health Review of Zoonotic and Reverse Zoonotic Diseases
作者： Sakarn Charoensakulchai, Nicha Nuntanajaroenkul, Pat Chantapinya, Manasvin Onwan
摘要中文翻译背景:国际旅行可以加速人畜共患病病原体的地理传播,并在某些情况下导致流行病或大流行的传播。旅行者还可能在旅游界面将人类病原体传播给野生动物,充当人畜共患和反向人畜共患 传播 的中介。方法:对存档的 WHO 和 ECDC 报告进行了筛查,以了解主要人畜共患病 暴发,其中国际旅行导致了跨境传播。在 PubMed 和 Scopus 中检索了有关人类与野生动物 传播 的研究,其中涉及游客、访客或可能促进 传播 的旅行者和旅行者行为。结果:在 14 种主要人畜共患 暴发 中,旅行者通常在最初的动物到人类 跨物种溢出 后充当传播者,将感染引入新地区,有时会导致进一步的 传播。这种模式在 SARS、MERS、流感和 COVID-19 等呼吸道感染以及 Oropouche 和 Zika 等 媒介 传播的感染中尤为突出。二十一项研究报告了人类与野生动物旅游界面可能或证实的反向人畜共患 传播。病原体包括呼吸道病毒、结核分枝杆菌、肠道病原体和耐药病原体。受影响的野生动物包括类人猿、灵长类动物、大象、猫科动物、食肉动物、有蹄类动物、鸟类、爬行动物以及国家公园、动物园、康复中心和半城市公园中适应城市的野生动物。另外五项研究记录了危险的游客行为,包括在出现呼吸道症状时近距离接近、身体接触、喂养和参观野生动物,以及针对 麻疹、流感和脊髓灰质炎的 麻疹 不足。结论:旅行者可以通过传播人畜共患病原体并使野生动物接触人类病原体来双向影响 传播。旅行医学应将有关距离、卫生、废物处理和免疫接种的野生动物保护咨询与传统的旅行者保护结合起来。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述研究了旅行者在人畜共患疾病和反向人畜共患疾病 传播 中的作用,重点是 麻疹病毒。它探讨了国际旅行如何促进病原体在人类和动物之间的传播,以及人类在野生动物接触面的行为如何可能导致人类病原体传播给野生动物。
证据基础与方法
该审查利用世卫组织和欧洲疾病预防控制中心存档的报告来确定涉及国际旅行的主要人畜共患病 暴发。它还在 PubMed 和 Scopus 上搜索有关人类与野生动物 传播 以及可能促进此类 传播 的旅行者行为的研究。
共识与结论
旅行者可以充当人畜共患病和反向人畜共患病 传播 的中介人。它们通常在最初的动物到人类 跨物种溢出 后将感染引入新区域,这可能导致后续的 传播。这种模式在 麻疹、流感等呼吸道感染中尤其明显。
争议与缺口
该评论指出,虽然有证据表明旅行者传播人畜共患病原体,但反向人畜共患 传播 的机制和程度仍未得到充分研究。此外,特定行为(例如喂养野生动物或 疫苗接种 不足)在促进 传播 中的作用尚未完全量化。
科研与实践启示
需要对旅行者行为在反向人畜共患病 传播 中的作用进行有针对性的研究。旅行医学应纳入有关距离、卫生、废物处理和免疫方面的野生动物保护咨询,以降低风险。

Travelers and Human–Animal Pathogen Transmission: A One Health Review of Zoonotic and Reverse Zoonotic Diseases

Authors: Sakarn Charoensakulchai, Nicha Nuntanajaroenkul, Pat Chantapinya, Manasvin Onwan
Original abstract (English)
Background: International travel can accelerate the geographic spread of zoonotic pathogens and, under certain circumstances, contribute to epidemic or pandemic dissemination. Travelers may also transmit human pathogens to wildlife at tourism interfaces, acting as intermediaries in zoonotic and reverse zoonotic transmission. Methods: Archived WHO and ECDC reports were screened for major zoonotic outbreaks in which international travel contributed to cross-border spread. PubMed and Scopus were searched for studies of human-to-wildlife transmission involving tourists, visitors, or travelers and traveler behaviors that could facilitate transmission. Results: Among 14 major zoonotic outbreaks, travelers commonly acted as spreaders following initial animal-to-human spillover by importing infections into new regions, sometimes resulting in onward transmission. This pattern was prominent with respiratory infections such as SARS, MERS, influenza, and COVID-19 and vector-borne infections such as Oropouche and Zika. Twenty-one studies reported possible or confirmed reverse zoonotic transmission at human–wildlife tourism interfaces. Pathogens included respiratory viruses, Mycobacterium tuberculosis, enteric pathogens, and antimicrobial-resistant pathogens. Affected wildlife included great apes, primates, elephants, felines, carnivorans, ungulates, avians, reptiles, and urban-adapted wildlife in national parks, zoological parks, rehabilitation centers, and semi-urban parks. Five additional studies documented risky tourist behaviors, including close approach to, physical contact with, and feeding and visiting wildlife while having respiratory symptoms, and inadequate vaccination against measles, influenza, and polio. Conclusions: Travelers can influence transmission bidirectionally by disseminating zoonotic pathogens and exposing wildlife to human pathogens. Travel medicine should incorporate wildlife-protective counseling on distancing, hygiene, waste disposal, and immunization alongside conventional traveler protection.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review examines the role of travelers in the transmission of zoonotic and reverse zoonotic diseases, with a focus on the measles virus. It explores how international travel can facilitate the spread of pathogens between humans and animals, and how human behaviors at wildlife interfaces may contribute to the transmission of human pathogens to wildlife.
Evidence base and method
The review utilized archived WHO and ECDC reports to identify major zoonotic outbreaks involving international travel. It also conducted searches on PubMed and Scopus for studies on human-to-wildlife transmission and traveler behaviors that could facilitate such transmission.
Consensus and conclusions
Travelers can act as intermediaries in both zoonotic and reverse zoonotic transmission. They commonly import infections into new regions following initial animal-to-human spillover, which can lead to onward transmission. This pattern is particularly evident with respiratory infections such as measles, influenza, and others.
Controversies and gaps
The review notes that while there is evidence of travelers spreading zoonotic pathogens, the mechanisms and extent of reverse zoonotic transmission remain underexplored. Additionally, the role of specific behaviors, such as feeding wildlife or inadequate vaccination, in facilitating transmission is not fully quantified.
Research and practice implications
There is a need for targeted research on the role of traveler behaviors in reverse zoonotic transmission. Travel medicine should incorporate wildlife-protective counseling on distancing, hygiene, waste disposal, and immunization to mitigate risks.

微阵列补丁 疫苗 能否有助于流行病防范?机会、实施考虑因素和优先事项

Could Microarray Patch Vaccines Contribute to Pandemic Preparedness? Opportunities, Implementation Considerations, and Priorities
作者： Matthew N. Berger
摘要中文翻译微阵列贴片 (MAP) 通过支持快速和大规模的 疫苗 分发和管理,显示出改善大流行病防备的潜力,特别是在冷链能力或卫生劳动力资源有限的环境中。这篇叙述性综述综合了临床前和临床 MAP 疫苗 研究以及有关产品开发、制造、监管、实施、可接受性和公平获取的文献,并报告了流感、日本脑炎、麻疹-风疹和 SARS-CoV-2 MAP 疫苗 的令人鼓舞的早期耐受性和免疫原性发现。显着的优点包括提高热稳定性、节省抗原剂量、简化应用、自我或非专业人士给药的可能性、减少对训练有素的疫苗接种人员的依赖以及适合外展和资源匮乏的环境。推进 MAP 技术需要选择靶点 疫苗、开发稳定的抗原特异性制剂、确认剂量输送、完成分阶段临床试验和监管评估、建立可扩展的、质量控制的制造系统,并整合到免疫计划中。挑战包括抗原相容性、剂量一致性、配方稳定性、生产规模扩大、监管要求、不确定的成本和商业激励、公众接受度和公平获取。获得许可后,将需要进行第四阶段 监测 和有效性研究来评估现实世界的安全性和性能。研究、制造、监管和交付方面的投资对于将 MAP 定位为传统 疫苗 的补充工具至关重要,从而扩大大流行免疫反应的速度、范围和灵活性。
查看综述五要素
范围与问题
该综述探讨了微阵列贴片 (MAP) 疫苗 在加强大流行防范方面的潜力,重点是 麻疹病毒。核心问题是 MAP 是否可以改善 疫苗 的分配和管理,特别是在冷链能力或卫生劳动力资源有限的资源匮乏环境中。
证据基础与方法
这篇叙述性综述综合了 MAP 疫苗 的临床前和临床研究,包括流感、日本脑炎、麻疹-风疹和 SARS-CoV-2 的研究。它还包含有关产品开发、制造、监管、实施、可接受性和公平获取的文献。该审查不报告具体数据库、检索日期或资格标准。
共识与结论
该综述强调,MAP 疫苗 对 麻疹 和其他相关病原体显示出令人鼓舞的早期耐受性和免疫原性发现。显着的优点包括提高热稳定性、节省抗原剂量、简化应用以及适合资源匮乏的环境。
争议与缺口
该审查确定了推进 MAP 技术的挑战,包括抗原兼容性、剂量一致性、配方稳定性、生产规模扩大、监管要求以及不确定的成本和商业激励。这些问题代表了需要进一步调查的证据差距。
科研与实践启示
获得许可后,审查建议需要进行 IV 期 监测 和有效性研究,以评估现实世界的安全性和性能。跨研究、制造、监管和交付的投资对于将 MAP 定位为传统 疫苗 的补充工具至关重要。

Could Microarray Patch Vaccines Contribute to Pandemic Preparedness? Opportunities, Implementation Considerations, and Priorities

Authors: Matthew N. Berger
Original abstract (English)
Microarray patches (MAPs) demonstrate potential to improve pandemic preparedness by supporting rapid and large-scale vaccine distribution and administration, particularly in settings with limited cold-chain capacity or health workforce resources. This narrative review synthesises preclinical and clinical MAP vaccine research alongside literature on product development, manufacturing, regulation, implementation, acceptability, and equitable access, and reports encouraging early-phase tolerability and immunogenicity findings for influenza, Japanese encephalitis, measles–rubella, and SARS-CoV-2 MAP vaccines. Notable advantages include improved thermostability, antigen dose sparing, simplified application, the possibility of self- or layperson administration, reduced reliance on trained vaccinators, and suitability for outreach and low-resource settings. Advancing MAP technology requires selection of target vaccines, development of stable, antigen-specific formulations, confirmation of dose delivery, completion of phased clinical trials and regulatory assessments, establishment of scalable, quality-controlled manufacturing systems, and integration into immunization programmes. Challenges involve antigen compatibility, dose consistency, formulation stability, manufacturing scale-up, regulatory requirements, uncertain costs and commercial incentives, public acceptance, and equitable access. Following licensure, Phase IV surveillance and effectiveness studies will be needed to evaluate real-world safety and performance. Investment across research, manufacturing, regulation, and delivery is essential to position MAPs as complementary tools to conventional vaccines, thus broadening the speed, reach, and flexibility of pandemic immunization responses.
View review five-element analysis
Scope and question
The review explores the potential of microarray patch (MAP) vaccines to enhance pandemic preparedness, with a focus on measles virus. The central question is whether MAPs can improve vaccine distribution and administration, particularly in low-resource settings with limited cold-chain capacity or health workforce resources.
Evidence base and method
This narrative review synthesizes preclinical and clinical research on MAP vaccines, including studies on influenza, Japanese encephalitis, measles–rubella, and SARS-CoV-2. It also incorporates literature on product development, manufacturing, regulation, implementation, acceptability, and equitable access. The review does not report specific databases, search dates, or eligibility criteria.
Consensus and conclusions
The review highlights that MAP vaccines show encouraging early-phase tolerability and immunogenicity findings for measles and other related pathogens. Notable advantages include improved thermostability, antigen dose sparing, simplified application, and suitability for low-resource settings.
Controversies and gaps
The review identifies challenges in advancing MAP technology, including antigen compatibility, dose consistency, formulation stability, manufacturing scale-up, regulatory requirements, and uncertain costs and commercial incentives. These issues represent evidence gaps that require further investigation.
Research and practice implications
Following licensure, the review suggests the need for Phase IV surveillance and effectiveness studies to evaluate real-world safety and performance. Investment across research, manufacturing, regulation, and delivery is essential to position MAPs as complementary tools to conventional vaccines.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

How Long Does the Measles Vaccine Last? What to Know About Measles in 2026 - University of Rochester

News Brief
The University of Rochester published an article on the measles vaccine's effectiveness and recommendations. It states that two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% protection, and most people vaccinated are protected for life. The article also recommends early doses for infants 6 to 11 months old traveling internationally or living in areas with measles outbreaks. Measles cases are surging in the United States, with over 5,000 documented cases this year.
Original source text (English)
Key Takeaways The MMR vaccine is highly effective against measles. One dose provides 93% protection, while two doses provide 97% protection. Most people who receive both doses are protected for life. One dose provides 93% protection, while two doses provide 97% protection. Most people who receive both doses are protected for life. Some children may need the measles vaccine earlier than usual. Infants ages 6 to 11 months may need an early dose if traveling internationally or visiting or living in an area experiencing a measles outbreak. if traveling internationally or visiting or living in an area experiencing a measles outbreak. Measles spreads easily through the air, even before symptoms appear. Watch for fever, cough, red eyes, and a spreading rash. If you suspect measles, call your healthcare provider before visiting a clinic to help prevent further spread. Measles cases are surging in 2025 and 2026, with over 5,000 documented cases in the United States this year. Most infections occur in unvaccinated individuals, but what about those who’ve been vaccinated? Here’s what you need to know about MMR vaccine effectiveness, symptoms to watch for, and how to keep your children and family safe. Update November, 11, 2026: The New York State Department of Health now recommends an early dose of the MMR vaccine for babies ages 6 to 11 months who are: traveling internationally, or visiting areas of the U.S. where measles is spreading, or living in or visiting a community experiencing a measles outbreak Before traveling, check the presence of measles in your destination and talk to your child’s pediatrician about whether an early MMR vaccine dose is recommended. Children 1 year and older should receive their second dose of MMR as soon as they are able to (28 days after the first MMR). How Long Does the Measles Vaccine Last? The measles vaccine is very effective, with two doses about 97% effective at preventing the disease if you are exposed to the virus. Most people vaccinated with both measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) doses are protected for life. Even if a vaccinated person does get measles, symptoms are usually much milder, and they’re less likely to spread it to others. Vaccination protects more than just you—it helps prevent the virus from spreading to newborns, people undergoing cancer treatment, and others who can't be vaccinated. Who may need another MMR dose? Adults born between 1963–1967 may need a modernized vaccine. Here’s how you can help: Make su re your family’s vaccinations are up to date Know your vaccine status—ask your healthcare provider Encourage others to get v accinated Adults born between 1963–1967 may need a modernized vaccine. Measles Vaccine Effectiveness Before vaccines, nearly every child in the U.S. got measles. Today, the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine is incredibly effective: 1 dose = 93% protection 2 doses = 97% protection What Is the Measles Vaccine Schedule for Children? Dr. Geoff Weinberg, a pediatrician and infectious disease expert at Golisano Children's, provides the latest recommendations for the measles vaccine in children. Children routinely receive two doses of the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine: First dose: 12 to 15 months old 12 to 15 months old Second dose: 4 to 6 years old Children who missed these routine vaccinations can still "catch up." They generally need two doses of the MMR vaccine given at least four weeks apart. Confirm with your pediatrician about the appropriate timing. Can Children Receive the MMR Vaccine Early? Children who have received both recommended doses generally do not need another measles vaccination. However, the schedule may change in certain circumstances: International travel: Infants ages 6 to 11 months should receive one dose of MMR before traveling internationally, visiting areas of the U.S. where measles is spreading, or living in or visiting a community experiencing a measles outbreak. Children 12 months and older should have two documented doses before international travel. Infants ages 6 to 11 months should receive one dose of MMR before traveling internationally, visiting areas of the U.S. where measles is spreading, or living in or visiting a community experiencing a measles outbreak. Children 12 months and older should have two documented doses before international travel. Measles outbreaks: Public health officials may recommend an early dose for infants ages 6 to 11 months who are at increased risk of exposure. Families should follow guidance from their care team and local public health officials. Public health officials may recommend an early dose for infants ages 6 to 11 months who are at increased risk of exposure. Families should follow guidance from their care team and local public health officials. Infants younger than 6 months: The MMR vaccine is not given to this age group. Why are there Measles Outbreaks? Measles is a highly contagious virus that spreads easily through the air. A single infected person can infect up to 90% of non-immune people around them. Vaccination is the best way to protect yourself and your community. “In a population that is not vaccinated, one person can infect another 12 to 18 people. That’s about a 90 percent attack rate and is honestly incredibly scary,” said Jennifer Nayak, MD, associate professor of pediatric infectious diseases with University of Rochester Medicine. Measles Symptoms to Watch for Unfortunately, only 88% of children in Monroe County are vaccinated by age two, falling short of the 95% needed for herd immunity. Measles can be severe—even deadly—especially for young children and people with weakened immune systems. Classic measles symptoms include: High fever Cough and runny nose Red, watery eyes (conjunctivitis) Rash that starts at the hairline and spreads down Measles can also lead to complications such as: Rare but fatal brain inflammation occurring either shortly following infection or even years afterward How Does Measles Spread? Measles is an airborne virus, meaning it spreads through tiny droplets in the air when someone coughs or sneezes. You can catch it just by being in the same room as an infected person, even long after they’ve left. “Measles can stay in the air and the room for up to two hours,” said Dr. Nayak. “That means if you’re going shopping an hour behind someone with measles, you can still potentially get infected. That’s why we’re scared of the contagiousness.” People with measles are contagious for about four days before the rash appears and four days after. That means they can spread the virus even before they know they’re sick. What To Do If You Suspect Measles If you or someone you know has symptoms, it’s essential to act quickly. If you or your child has symptoms like fever and rash, call your doctor before going to any clinic. This allows your provider to deliver care to you in the safest way possible, helping prevent the virus from spreading further, including to infants and others who are unable to be vaccinated. Measles and infants: How parents can protect babies from this highly contagious disease - UCHealth

News Brief
Measles cases in the U.S. have surged to over 5,182 in 2026, more than double the 2025 count. Infants are particularly vulnerable, with at least two under-one-year-olds having died from the disease. Experts advise parents to vaccinate older babies and extend breastfeeding for younger infants to protect against measles, a highly contagious illness.
Original source text (English)

Clinical trials Employees Professionals Media inquiries Financial assistance Contact us News & stories Help me find Doctors Specialties Locations Schedule My Health Connection Log in UCHealth Today All Innovative care Healthy living Stories News Live Colorado Español UCHealth Today > Measles and infants: How parents can protect babies from this highly contagious disease As measles infections rise in Colorado and the U.S., experts explain why infants are vulnerable and how families can protect newborns and young children. By: Joanna Bean Yesterday Infants are among the most vulnerable to measles because they are not routinely vaccinated until 12 months of age. Photo: Getty Images. Amid an increase in measles infections in the U.S., including a northern Colorado infant who contracted the highly infectious illness while the family traveled abroad, parents may have questions about how to keep their babies safe. Infants are among the most vulnerable to measles because they are not routinely vaccinated until 12 months of age. That means their protection against measles depends heavily on the immunity of people around them. In the early 2000s, U.S. health officials declared measles eradicated. But measles cases are on the rise in Colorado and elsewhere after declining vaccination rates. That, along with the annual winter season of cold, flu, RSV and other illnesses, means babies are particularly at risk of getting sick. To answer parents’ questions about keeping newborns and babies safe from measles and other wintertime illnesses, we consulted with two UCHealth doctors: Dr. Megan Ross, a pediatrician with UCHealth Estes Valley Medical Center, and Dr. Michael Ramos, a family medicine doctor with UCHealth Family Medicine – Parkview Pueblo West. Here’s what they recommend to keep newborns healthy and what families should do if they suspect they may have been exposed to measles or another illness and need medical care. What is measles, and why are cases rising in Colorado and the U.S.? Measles is one of the most contagious diseases in the world, Ross said. Infected people typically get sick with symptoms similar to those of a cold or the flu, namely fever, runny nose and red eyes. Then, a rash appears on the top of the body and moves downward. Symptoms typically appear seven to 14 days after exposure, and it can take up to 21 days for an infected person to get sick. How can parents prevent measles in newborns and older babies? Parents should vaccinate their babies to prevent measles once the baby turns a year old. Infants can get the measles vaccine as young as age six months if babies and their parents will be traveling to areas with measles outbreaks. Babies under the age of six months can’t get measles vaccines, so family members should protect babies from illnesses by staying up to date on their vaccines. Mothers can pass immunity to measles and other illnesses through their breast milk. Extended breastfeeding can help protect babies from getting sick. Sick people should stay away from newborns. Parents should make sure all visitors wash their hands carefully before visiting newborns. Do not allow visitors to kiss their baby on the head or face. Measles is one of the most contagious diseases on Earth, and nine of 10 unvaccinated people who are exposed to measles will get it. Babies and older people who get measles can get very sick and can die from measles infections, and people who get measles also can suffer long-term health problems. Infected people can spread measles without knowing it because they can be contagious before having any symptoms. The virus can remain in the air for up to two hours after an infected person leaves the area. Measles is especially dangerous because it spreads so easily. Researchers have found that nine of 10 people who are not vaccinated and get exposed to the measles virus will get infected. “The outbreaks are so difficult to get a handle on because not everybody who is contagious has that stereotypical high fever and the rash,” Ross said. “They can look and feel totally fine, and those virus particles that they breathe out can hang in the air for two hours. Anybody nearby who isn’t vaccinated and protected against measles is then at risk for contracting measles.” That’s why so-called herd immunity is so important, Ross said. Herd immunity refers to having a high enough percentage of people vaccinated to prevent sustained transmission of measles. Herd immunity of 95% is required to stop a measles outbreak. But herd immunity for measles has waned in recent years as fewer children have received the vaccine, in part because of false information about vaccines that have left communities vulnerable to outbreaks. In Colorado, 90.9% of people ages one to 18 have received at least one dose of the MMR vaccine, which provides combined protection for measles, mumps and rubella, according to medical experts at the Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment. How measles creates immune amnesia in your body. The number of measles cases across the U.S. this year has surged to more than double the number of cases in 2025. As of Nov. 6, health officials at the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) confirmed 5,182 measles cases across the country so far in 2026. That’s more than double the 2,289 confirmed in 2025. And at least two babies under age one have died from measles this year, with one confirmed death of an infant in Pennsylvania and another in Wisconsin. In Colorado, health officials confirmed 27 measles cases so far in 2026, compared to 36 cases in all of 2025. The Colorado cases include an infant in Weld County who contracted measles. The baby’s family sought care at multiple locations in the area in late October. Why is measles dangerous for infants and babies? Newborns and older people face higher risks of complications from measles if they contract it, Ross said. “When measles affects a kiddo, it makes them very sick,” she said. “They have a one in five chance of needing to be admitted to the hospital.” Complications of measles for babies include developing ear infections, viral pneumonia and encephalitis, or inflammation of the brain, which can cause seizures, loss of hearing and permanent brain damage. Parents should check with their medical provider as soon as possible any time a newborn has a fever. For a newborn with a fever, doctors typically order bloodwork and a urine test and may even need to do a spinal tap to determine what is causing the fever, Ross said. “With babies that young, we have to be very proactive in identifying where their infection is,” she said. Do babies have any protection against measles? Newborns under the age of six months generally have some immunity to measles that they received while in utero, if their mother was vaccinated or previously contracted measles. But maternal protection varies and wanes over time. Babies depend upon herd immunity to stay safe from the disease, she said. Can mothers pass immunities to babies through breast milk? Yes. Mothers who are breastfeeding their babies can pass immunities that can prevent measles and other illnesses like the flu and colds to their babies through their milk. That’s why Ramos encourages all mothers to breastfeed, if possible, and to continue breastfeeding infants as long as possible to extend the period of time during which they’ll receive immunities from their mothers. When can babies get the measles vaccines? Babies typically get their first dose of the measles vaccine at 12 months of age, as part of the MMR vaccine. Children typically get the second dose, also called a booster, around age four to six, though children can get the second dose of measles vaccine as soon as 29 days after their first dose, Ross said. Parents who plan to travel with their infant to an area with a high rate of measles infections can get their baby a dose of the MMR vaccine when the baby is six months old. The child still will require two additional doses. Officials with the Colorado Department of Public Health and Environment recommend that babies age 6 months and older get the vaccine if they are traveling internationally. Babies under age six months cannot get the measles vaccine. One downside to getting the measles vaccine before age 12 months and then getting two more doses is that a child’s immunity is not quite as high as it would be with the typical vaccine at 12 months, Ross said. The Weld County infant who contracted measles was not old enough for the routine MMR vaccination and the parents hadn’t opted for the optional vaccine at six months of age. Is the measles vaccine safe? “The MMR vaccine is unequivocally safe,” Ross said. “It does not cause autism. And the single best way you can protect your child from getting measles is to vaccinate on time.” Ramos said most of the parents he sees in his practice want to vaccinate their children for measles as soon as possible. “I’m actually seeing an influx across all age groups regarding MMR immunization,” he said. How can I protect my baby from measles? All family members, older siblings, caregivers and visitors need to be up to date on their vaccines, Ramos said. Anyone who hasn’t been vaccinated yet, including a sibling, can get a measles vaccine and then a booster 30 days later and be fully immunized. How can I protect my baby from measles while traveling? When it comes to traveling, Ross recommends avoiding places where measles outbreaks are occurring, if possible. Unfortunately, that is becoming more difficult since measles outbreaks are widespread throughout the U.S. this year and around the world. Parents can get information about outbreaks through local health departments and the CDC. Overall, she suggests evaluating the risks and benefits of a trip. Get more advice on traveling and measles. “I really don’t want people to stop living their lives,” she said. “We still need to go to that story time at the library, and it might be important to go visit grandma out of state. I encourage people to be thoughtful about the risks and benefits of every action, and as long as we mitigate the risks, including getting vaccinated when it’s time to get vaccinated, we can enjoy all the things we hope to.” How do I know if my baby has measles, and what should I do? Early measles symptoms in a child resemble those of a cold and include fever, cough, red eyes and a runny nose. About four days after those symptoms begin, someone with measles typically develops a rash of little red dots that starts at the hairline and spreads down the body. One tip-off that the illness might be measles is the combination of a fever of 103-104 degrees and the start of a rash, she said. If you suspect your baby might have measles, call your healthcare provider before going to their office or to urgent care or the emergency room, Ross said. A nurse or other provider can help assess the baby’s symptoms and determine if the baby needs care, and where. The provider also can call ahead to the facility to ensure that healthcare providers have the right protocols in place to prevent infections from spreading to healthy patients. What should I do if I think my baby has been exposed to measles? Ross recommends parents call or send a message to their pediatrician or healthcare provider if they think their baby has been exposed to measles. Together, they can determine if the baby has symptoms and needs treatment. How do doctors treat measles in babies and children? The treatment for infants who get infected with measles focuses on making sure they don’t become dehydrated, managing their fever and monitoring babies for complications such as pneumonia. Doctors may give Vitamin A to children who have measles, especially those sick enough to be admitted to the hospital, as part of guidelines of the World Health Organization and the American Academy of Pediatrics, Ross said. Vitamin A will not kill the virus nor cure the complications, but it can help with a child’s recovery, especially for children who are deficient in Vitamin A. Cod liver oil is not a treatment for measles, she said. Cod liver oil and Vitamin A will not prevent measles. Is it safe to take my baby to the doctor during a measles outbreak? Ross recommends making an appointment earlier in the day, when fewer families are in the waiting room or examination room. Flu season hits early in Colorado. Learn how to keep your child healthier during cold and flu season. All healthcare providers have protocols to help ensure their facilities are as safe as possible for children and their parents. Above all, Ross urges parents to bring their children to their doctor appointments, even if they’re afraid of contracting an illness in the doctor’s office. “When folks decline or delay bringing their kids in for well-child care, we know that that negatively impacts them,” she said. “The number of kids who were behind on their vaccinations coming through the COVID-19 pandemic was just astounding. Coming in for well-child care and making sure your kids are developmentally meeting milestones are really important reasons to come to your appointment, if at all possible.” How can I protect my baby from flu, COVID-19, RSV and other illnesses? Ross encourages parents to feel empowered to remind family and friends that their baby’s immune system isn’t as strong as an older child’s, and to visit only if they’re healthy. “Anybody with any kind of illness should be masked or wait until feeling better before seeing a newborn,” she said. “For little ones, I would be very protective of who sees them.” Ramos encourages parents to practice good hand-washing hygiene to avoid passing along illness to their baby. He suggests that parents avoid large crowds and never let anyone kiss their baby on the face or head. Staying up to date on all recommended age-appropriate vaccines is important, he said. What else should parents know about childhood vaccines? Vaccines do not cause autism, SIDS and other major problems, research shows. Most children who get vaccines experience soreness at the injection site. More serious reactions occur so rarely that the benefits of vaccination far exceed the risks. Babies can safely receive multiple vaccinations at the same time as part of routine vaccine protocols. Most newborns get the hepatitis B vaccine soon after birth, before the infant leaves the hospital. Babies do carry immunity from their mothers, but those immunities decline gradually after birth. Mothers who breastfeed their babies can pass along immunities for measles and other illnesses through their breast milk. Flu shots are recommended for babies, children and adults who are six months of age and older. Babies and children should receive vaccines according to the standard schedule endorsed by doctors and researchers with the American Academy of Pediatrics, the American Academy of Family Physicians and other experts. US poised to lose measles elimination status—what does that mean? - CIDRAP

News Brief
The United States is facing the risk of losing its measles elimination status, with 2 recent deaths from measles complications and over 5,400 cases reported. The situation is under investigation, with an international body meeting to consider the evidence of measles being endemic in the US.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

The U.S. Has Lost Its Measles-Elimination Status, Data Suggests - The New York Times

News Brief
The U.S. has lost its measles-elimination status, as confirmed by genetic data analysis and an upcoming evaluation by an international panel of scientific experts. Over the past two years, nearly 5,200 measles cases have been reported, with eight deaths this year, more than the combined deaths from 2000 through 2024. Measles is now endemic in the U.S., with several chains of transmission continuing for over a year. An international panel will evaluate the status on Nov. 14, 2026, and the U.S. will need to increase immunization rates and reduce pockets of vulnerability to regain elimination status.
Original source text (English)
Supported by The U.S. Has Lost Its Measles-Elimination Status, Data Suggests An international panel of scientific experts will convene on Tuesday to evaluate the status, but genetic data already shows the virus is once again entrenched in the United States. Measles is once again endemic — a constant and predictable presence — in the United States, 26 years after it was declared eliminated in the country, an analysis of genetic data from outbreaks of the disease has found. Data from the outbreaks will be the focus of deliberations by an international panel of scientific experts convening on Tuesday to decide whether the United States should lose its official designation as a nation that has eliminated measles. An announcement of the decision is expected on Nov. 17. The New York Times asked several scientists to analyze the publicly available genetic data before the meeting. Achieving elimination status in 2000 was a hard-fought public health victory for the United States. But with skepticism about the safety of vaccines growing in recent years, outbreaks have become more frequent. Over the past two years, cases have soared, with nearly 5,200 reported so far this year. Last week alone, Pennsylvania, New York and Wisconsin each reported a death associated with measles, bringing this year’s total to eight, more than the combined deaths from 2000 through 2024. To officially lose elimination status, a committee of the Pan American Health Organization, a branch of the World Health Organization, must determine that the country has had uninterrupted transmission of measles for more than 12 months. Publicly available data shows that several chains of transmission have continued for more than a year in the United States, according to scientists who analyzed the data at the request of The New York Times. Three chains that were continuing in July 2026 — two in Utah and one in Arizona — can all be traced to an outbreak in Texas more than a year earlier. One of those, which was still continuing in Utah in September, had been circulating for over a year in that state, occasionally spilling over into Oregon and Washington. “This alone is strong evidence that there has been ongoing transmission for over a year,” said Bill Hanage, a co-director of the Center for Communicable Disease Dynamics at the Harvard T.H. Chan School of Public Health. Another transmission chain detected in Virginia at the start of the year was still spreading in Iowa, Indiana and Pennsylvania as of mid-October. (There is often a delay of several weeks before genetic data is available online.) “There’s a general pattern of outbreaks seeding other outbreaks,” said J.T. McCrone, an evolutionary biologist at the Fred Hutch Cancer Center who helps oversee Nextstrain, the publicly available database that contains genetic information for measles and many other pathogens. “Measles is very contagious; it spreads very easily,” Dr. McCrone said. “We have a lot of connected communities in the U.S., and so you can hop from population to population and maintain a transmission chain.” Some critics consider the potential loss of elimination status a referendum on Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who has for decades questioned the safety of vaccines in general and the measles vaccine in particular. He has falsely said that the measles vaccine can be fatal, that it contains fetal debris and that its effectiveness plummets over time. None of the eight Americans who died from measles this year had been vaccinated against the disease. (Four were infants who were too young for the shot.) Mr. Kennedy has repeatedly noted that measles is on the rise in many countries beyond the United States. Due in large part to a rise in vaccine hesitancy and misinformation since the pandemic, seven countries, including Canada, Spain and Britain, have lost their elimination status in the past two years. Mexico is expected to lose it this month along with the United States. But the explosion of cases in the United States has coincided precisely with his time in office. Last year, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recorded nearly 2,300 cases and three deaths. Most cases of measles in the years since elimination were linked to travel abroad, particularly in countries where the disease is still endemic. But those importations did not spread widely and died out quickly because more than 95 percent of children in the United States had been immunized against the disease. That percentage dropped to 92.4 percent in the school year beginning in 2025, leaving nearly 300,000 kindergartners at risk. Rising vaccine hesitancy has led to sharp increases in parents seeking exemptions for their children from school vaccination requirements. The Covid pandemic exacerbated the problem, first by derailing routine immunizations and then by intensifying mistrust of vaccines. Mr. Kennedy’s statements from his pulpit as the country’s top health official also fueled misplaced fears that childhood vaccines might be more dangerous than the diseases they are intended to prevent. Regaining elimination status will require more than ending the current transmission chains, experts said. Public health officials will need to increase immunization rates across the country and cut down on the number of pockets into which the virus can jump. The measles virus evolves slowly, allowing the vaccine to be effective even without frequent updates. But its slow evolution also means that pinpointing how the virus is changing or moving requires deeper analyses. Scientists have typically relied on snippets of the measles virus, which was enough to identify the sporadic cases in the country. But they now have the technology to decode the full genetic sequences of the samples, giving them clearer insight into how the virus is changing and moving, and enabling them to distinguish endemic circulation from travel-associated cases. “If this outbreak had occurred in 2006, we wouldn’t have the technology to be able to do this,” said Robert Potter, a clinical microbiologist at the Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia. “We never really had to study measles from a genomic perspective before because there was no measles to sequence,” he added. “It’s giving us a lot of new information, but it’s information that we didn’t necessarily need to have.” Apoorva Mandavilli reports on science and global health for The Times, with a focus on infectious diseases and pandemics and the public health agencies that try to manage them. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases surge in US, threaten decades of progress - Spotlight PA

News Brief
Measles cases are surging in the U.S. and Mexico, with over 5,400 confirmed cases and more than 50 deaths, mostly in children under 5. Experts are meeting to determine if the U.S. and Mexico have lost their measles elimination status. The CDC will continue its evidence-based approach to address outbreaks, but public health experts advocate for increased vaccination campaigns.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content News Economy Elections Environment Health Investigations Josh Shapiro Justice System Local Government Penn State Restaurant Inspections Rural Issues State Capitol More Topics Archives Bureaus State College Berks County Newsletters PA Post Good Day, Berks Talk of the Town PA Local The Investigator Access Harrisburg Games Crossword Mini Crossword News Quiz Slide Sudoku Word Flower Word Search WordroW About Our Mission Meet the Team Board of Directors Spotlight PA Partners Impact Events Careers Mobile Apps Contact Send a Tip Advertise With Us Donate Ways to Give Manage Your Membership How We're Funded Leaders in Action Spotlight PA Store Agentic Giving Menu Menu Newsletters Donate Search Journalism that Gets Results SM Investigations State College Berks County About Main content Health The US and Mexico are on the verge of forfeiting a major health victory. Here’s what to know by Mike Stobbe of the Associated Press | Nov. 13, 2026 Marco Ugarte / Associated Press Share: NEW YORK (AP) — International health experts are meeting this week to determine if the United States and Mexico have forfeited one of their greatest public health victories — the elimination of measles. Measles is one of the most contagious diseases known to medicine, but vaccines had safely stopped the virus’ spread in the U.S. for 30 years. Over those decades, measles had become a medical rarity in the U.S., with deaths from the disease virtually unheard of. But in the past two years, large outbreaks have hit multiple states, spawning thousands of illnesses, including about a dozen deaths. Experts expect that the meeting, convened by the Pan American Health Organization, or PAHO, will show that measles has officially returned to the U.S. and Mexico — that the virus is now continually spreading within the nations’ borders. “It’s a big deal. The decision represents us moving backward,” said Dr. David Margolius, director of Cleveland’s health department and a leader in a coalition of big U.S. city health departments. Here’s a look at the meeting’s deliberations and why they are important. International health experts are poring over measles data this week In a closed-door meeting this week, a PAHO advisory commission is looking at genetic evidence to determine if the same measles virus has been spreading for one year or more in the outbreaks that have torn through North America starting in 2024. “It’s solely based on genetics,” said Dr. Amesh Adalja, an infectious disease doctor in the Pittsburgh area and a senior scholar at the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security. If so, the commission will say the country can no longer be considered to have eliminated measles, meaning a return to sustained circulation of the virus. Last year, PAHO stripped Canada of the measles-free designation it had had since 1998. The commission will present its determination to PAHO Director Dr. Jarbas Barbosa, a former Brazilian health official, who makes the final determination. An announcement of the findings is being scheduled for next week, PAHO officials said. The CDC says loss of measles elimination status won’t change its approach A PAHO decision to strip the U.S. of its measles elimination status will not change how federal health officials are addressing outbreaks, CDC Director Erica Schwartz said this week. “The measles elimination designation is not what protects Americans. Vaccination does. We know where transmission is occurring and we’re working with state and local health departments to reach those communities. Losing a designation doesn’t mean we abandon an evidence-based approach. It means we remain focused on stopping outbreaks,” she said in the statement. But many public health experts say such an occasion demands a stepped-up, full-throated campaign to promote vaccinations and provide more advice to doctors. They fault what they say is a politically fraught, muted federal response, and blame U.S. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who was a leading voice in the anti-vaccine movement before President Donald Trump put him in charge of the nation’s health agencies. Kennedy has moved to downsize the childhood vaccination schedule, reshape a key vaccine advisory board, and laud Trump’s executive order calling for spacing out the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine. Such a move runs counter to medical groups’ recommendations, and likely would result in fewer children getting fully vaccinated, experts say. The US is having its worst measles year in decades Measles typically begins with a high fever followed by a telltale rash that starts on the face and neck. Most people recover, but it’s one of the leading causes of death among young children, according to the World Health Organization. Serious complications, including blindness and swelling of the brain, are more common in young children and adults older than 30. The U.S. achieved measles elimination status in 2000, after decades of vaccination campaigns brought the childhood measles vaccination rate to above 97%. Health experts say a 95% vaccination rate is needed so that when an infected traveler enters a community, so many people are vaccinated that it will not spark an outbreak. But U.S. vaccination rates have been slipping for years, in part because of disproven claims that the vaccine can cause autism. Trump claimed earlier this year, without evidence, that the MMR vaccine — which is credited with saving millions of lives globally — is possibly “quite lethal.” Last year, the childhood vaccination rate fell to about 92% nationally, and was much lower in certain states and certain communities. Measles exploded last year in large outbreaks in Texas, Arizona, Utah and South Carolina. And the more than 5,400 confirmed cases so far this year are already double the 2025 tally, driven largely by an outbreak in Pennsylvania that has dwarfed the earlier ones. The U.S. has now seen eight measles-associated deaths this year — more than any year in more than three decades — and is enduring its worst year for measles illnesses since 1991. At least 50 people have died from measles in Mexico A measles vaccine did not become available in Mexico until years after a measles vaccine was first licensed — in 1963, in the U.S. — and the U.S. started giving shots. Mexico did not achieve measles elimination status until 2016. Cases began surging in Mexico early last year. Researchers think an outbreak in the northern border state of Chihuahua stemmed from an unvaccinated Mennonite child from that Mexican state who traveled to Gaines County, Texas, where an outbreak was occurring, and came back infected. As of this week, more than 20,500 confirmed cases have been reported across the nation. Fifty measles-related deaths have been reported, most of them children younger than 5 years old. The issue has not been confined to North America. Early this year, the World Health Organization confirmed that Armenia, Austria, Azerbaijan, Spain, the United Kingdom and Uzbekistan lost their status. ___ Associated Press writer Megan Janetsky contributed to this story from Mexico City. ___ The Associated Press’ health and science coverage receives financial support from the AP Fund for Journalism and private foundations. AP is solely responsible for all content. Find AP’s standards for working with philanthropies, a list of supporters and funded coverage areas at AP.org. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wisconsin reports 2nd measles death as outbreak reaches 649 cases

News Brief
Wisconsin has reported a second death linked to a measles outbreak, bringing the total number of cases to 649 across 20 counties since August. Health officials are urging vaccination, with over 45,600 MMR doses administered and nearly 2,000 more delivered to affected communities. The outbreak, the highest in Wisconsin in over 35 years, highlights the importance of vaccination to prevent further spread and protect vulnerable populations.
Original source text (English)
Wisconsin reports 2nd measles death as outbreak reaches 649 cases By WDIO Measles Vaccine (MMR). (CBS Evening News) In Brief Wisconsin reports a second child death linked to the ongoing 2026 measles outbreak, with 649 cases across 20 counties since August. Health officials are urging vaccination, with over 45,600 MMR doses given and nearly 2,000 more delivered to affected communities. Residents are advised to check vaccination records, stay home if exposed, and contact health care providers if symptoms develop. A second child has died from measles in Wisconsin as the outbreak continues, according to the Wisconsin Department of Health Services (DHS) and the Grant County Health Department. To protect the privacy of the family and child, no further details about the death will be released. RELATED: Wisconsin’s first death of measles reported since 1990 Measles is a highly contagious virus that spreads through the air and close contact. It can cause life-threatening complications, especially for children under 5, pregnant people and those with weakened immune systems. As of November 13, 2026, there have been 649 measles cases in 20 counties since August, the highest number in Wisconsin in more than 35 years. DHS noted that actual case numbers may be higher, as not everyone seeks medical care or gets tested. RELATED: Measles cases in Minnesota hit highest total in 36 years DHS response and vaccination efforts DHS continues to work with local and Tribal health departments and health care partners to coordinate statewide response efforts. These include case tracking, information sharing, testing and vaccination. Since August 1, 2026, nearly 2,000 doses of MMR vaccine have been delivered to communities with confirmed cases, and over 45,600 total doses have been administered by local agencies and health care providers. DHS has also provided support to prevent wider spread in communities and health care settings. Prevention and public health advice “Preventing the spread of measles takes action from all of us,” said State Health Officer and Division of Public Health Administrator Paula Tran. “From making sure our families are protected by getting the recommended vaccinations to staying home when we may have been exposed, we encourage everyone to work together to protect those who are most vulnerable to serious illness. Stay informed, look out for one another, and take the actions you can to help stop the spread of measles and keep our communities healthy and safe.” DHS urges everyone to be proactive by getting the measles, mumps, rubella (MMR) vaccine, which is about 97% effective with two doses. Wisconsin residents can check their vaccination records online using the Wisconsin Immunization Registry or by contacting their health care provider or local health department. If exposed to measles, DHS advises people to stay home. Anyone who develops a fever and rash, especially if unvaccinated or exposed, should contact their health care provider and call ahead before visiting a clinic or hospital. For more information about measles in Wisconsin, including vaccination recommendations and current case activity, residents can visit the DHS measles webpage

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases nationwide top 5,400 as outbreaks spread across the US

News Brief
The United States has reported a total of 5,412 measles cases across 45 states, with 10 deaths. This marks a significant increase from the previous update, with 230 new cases reported. Vaccination rates among kindergartners have dropped to 92%, below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity. The measles virus, highly contagious and potentially fatal, has infected 300,000 unvaccinated kindergartners, with severe complications including ear infections, diarrhea, and pneumonia. Health officials are investigating and implementing prevention measures, with a decision on the U.S. measles elimination status expected soon.

Related What parents need to know as measles outbreaks spread across the US The measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine comes in two doses. The first dose is received between 12-15 months of age, followed by a second dose between 4-6 years of age. Those two doses are 97% effective at preventing infection. Even if still infected, those vaccinated are less likely to become severely ill. More on measles Measles in the most contagious disease in the world. A sick individual can infect 90% of unvaccinated people they come into contact with. The virus lives on surfaces and in the air two hours after a sick person leaves the room. Signs point the way to measles testing in the parking lot of the Seminole Hospital District across from Wigwam Stadium on February 27, 2025 in Seminole, Texas. Jan Sonnenmair/Getty Images, FILE Measles is not just a rash. One in 10 children with measles get ear infections and one in 10 children with measles get diarrhea. One in 5 people that are unvaccinated land in the hospital, while one in 20 children get pneumonia. Popular Reads Transit officials trot out video of moment 2 horses boarded public bus in Detroit 8 hours ago Ukraine fires nearly 1,200 drones at Russia as US-led talks consider Europe security 14 hours ago Avril Lavigne announces birth of 1st child 9 hours ago Additionally, one in 1,000 children get encephalitis, a swelling of the brain that can lead to seizures, deafness, of intellectual disability. Nearly one to three of every 1,000 children who become infected die from respiratory and neurological complications. B routine priorityPublic-health news

CDC reports six measles deaths in 2026 as US battles worst outbreak in 35 years - The Guardian

News Brief
The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported six measles-related deaths in the US in 2026, marking the worst outbreak in 35 years with over 5,400 cases reported nationwide. Pennsylvania and New York have been the most severely affected states, with the CDC now using data from the National Center for Health Statistics (NCHS) to track measles fatalities. The US could lose its measles elimination status this week, a development likely to further define Robert F Kennedy Jr's legacy of interference in vaccine policies.

Photograph: Juan Diego Reyes for The Washington Post via Getty Images View image in fullscreen Medical providers assess a patient with measles symptoms in South Carolina in January. Photograph: Juan Diego Reyes for The Washington Post via Getty Images CDC reports six measles deaths in 2026 as US battles worst outbreak in 35 years Figures highlight spread of virus, officially declared eliminated in US in 2000, but CDC does not identify which states accounted for deaths The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) reported six measles-related deaths in the US this year, according to figures published on its website on Thursday, as the country faces its most severe resurgence of the disease in decades. The newly reported total is twice the number of deaths recorded in 2025, highlighting the spread of the highly infectious virus, which was officially declared eliminated in the US in 2000. The country is currently experiencing its largest measles outbreak since 1991. More than 5,400 cases reported nationwide, according to data from the Johns Hopkins University International Vaccine Access Center tracker. Pennsylvania has been the state most severely affected by the outbreak, with officials confirming six measles-associated deaths in the state this year. A child also died in New York on election day, followed days later by the death of an infant in Wisconsin. Active outbreaks have been reported in nearly every US state. The Wisconsin department of health services confirmed the infant’s death, while New York recorded its first known measles-related fatality in more than three decades. The CDC did not identify which states accounted for the six deaths included in its official count. The difference in the reported figures followed a change in how the CDC, operating under US health secretary Robert F Kennedy Jr, tracks measles fatalities. The CDC is now using data from the National Center for Health Statistics (NCHS), which is a way to track mortality trends over time, according to a note published to the agency’s website on 4 September. Kennedy previously argued without evidence that two of the deaths in Pennsylvania may not be from measles. He asked Erica Schwartz, the CDC’s director, not to count the deaths in the official CDC tally, according to reporting by Reuters. The day after the Reuters report was published, the CDC changed its data source on measles deaths. Measles is preventable through the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine, which offers approximately 97% protection after two doses. The US could lose its measles elimination status this week, a development likely to further define Kennedy ’s legacy of interference in vaccine policies. Losing that designation would place the US alongside Canada and several countries in Europe and Asia where measles had previously been eliminated before making a comeback. With 麻疹 surging,the US is likely to lose 'elimination' status. 疫苗 提供最好的希望

With measles surging, the US is likely to lose 'elimination' status. Vaccines offer best hope
