B routine priorityPublic-health news

With measles surging, the US is likely to lose 'elimination' status. Vaccines offer best hope

News Brief
Measles cases in the U.S. are surging, with over 5,100 cases reported in 2026, the highest in 35 years. The disease is highly contagious and has led to six deaths. The U.S. is likely to lose its 'elimination' status, which means the country has not seen very large outbreaks and has not had uncontrolled domestic transmission for a year or more. Experts recommend increased vaccination to control the spread.
Original source text (English)
With measles surging, the US is likely to lose 'elimination' status. Vaccines offer best hope Advertisement Updated: 12:14 PM MST Nov 13, 2026 Editorial Standards i By Madison Czopek, PolitiFact's Well Informed With measles surging, the US is likely to lose 'elimination' status. Vaccines offer best hope Updated: 12:14 PM MST Nov 13, 2026 Editorial Standards i By Madison Czopek, PolitiFact's Well Informed Babies are dying in Wisconsin, Pennsylvania, and New York. A high school closed in Pennsylvania. A Texas labor and delivery unit had to enact emergency protocols after measles was detected in a laboring mother. This article originally appeared on PolitiFact.com's Well Informed . Advertisement Measles is spreading in the U.S. at a pace not seen in over three decades. The highly contagious respiratory disease can cause a rash, cough, runny nose, fever, watery eyes and high fevers. It can lead to hospitalization and death. With communities nationwide feeling the disease’s effects, the U.S. is on track to lose the sought-after "measles elimination" status it achieved 26 years ago. The Pan American Health Organization, a regional office of the World Health Organization that coordinates public health efforts across dozens of countries, designates nations as having achieved measles elimination when robust surveillance shows they have ended " endemic " — or regular and sustained — virus transmission. Elimination status means a country has not seen very large measles outbreaks and has not had uncontrolled domestic virus transmission for a year or more. This is no longer true of the United States, data shows . Pennsylvania measles cases reach more than 1,000. Track outbreaks in your state Measles cases skyrocketed in 2025 and 2026. This year alone, the country has already recorded more than 5,100 measles cases — the highest number seen in 35 years. With case counts rising each week, experts say there’s only one way to end the spread: increased measles vaccination. Any community with vaccine coverage below 95% is particularly vulnerable to outbreaks. Measles is "exquisitely contagious," said Jennifer Nuzzo, a Brown University epidemiology professor who tracks measles . "The virus just has to show up, and then it explodes." How many measles cases have U.S. health authorities recorded this year? The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention had reported 5,182 measles cases and six deaths in 2026 as of Nov. 6. The CDC says it counts only confirmed cases . Other public health data aggregators have recorded higher case counts. Johns Hopkins’ U.S. Measles Tracker reported 5,400 cases in 2026 as of Nov. 11. That total reflects lab-confirmed measles cases compiled from state and county health departments, county-level news releases and news articles. Wisconsin infant dies amid measles outbreak, the third measles-related death this week in the US Brown University School of Public Health’s Tracking Report newsletter tallied 5,478 cases and nine deaths as of Nov. 5 — eight confirmed by states and one confirmed by a local coroner. Its report pulls data from state health department websites and counts cases by the dates they were reported to health organizations. Other organizations often mark the cases by the date the measles rash is detected. How many measles outbreaks have been documented this year? With two months left in the year, the CDC reports 43 new measles outbreaks in 2026, defining “outbreak” as three or more related cases. The agency reported 48 outbreaks total last year and 16 in 2024. The U.S. has recorded more than 100 new measles cases each week for 13 weeks straight . How many states are seeing large measles outbreaks? So far in 2026, 13 states have seen more than 100 measles cases, according to CDC data. In 2025, just five states surpassed 100 cases. At least six states are currently battling sizable measles outbreaks. Pennsylvania’s outbreak is the largest this year by far among all states, with officials documenting more than 1,500 cases and six measles-associated deaths, according to state health department data as of Nov. 9. Hundreds more cases have been documented in South Carolina, Utah and Wisconsin. Not everyone infected with measles seeks medical care, so these figures are likely undercounts . And states often record a measles-associated death if the person died while having measles. Grandparents urged to get measles, flu, and other shots before meeting a newborn in the U.S. Under the second Trump administration, the CDC has not recorded the same number of U.S. measles deaths as states, after Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr., an antivaccine activist, publicly questioned whether evidence sufficiently demonstrated measles was the cause of death. In the U.S., officials typically expect about one to two deaths per 1,000 measles cases. Given the nine measles-associated deaths states have reported this year, "there is reason to believe that we are far, far from totally enumerating the amount of infections," Nuzzo said. Why is the U.S. likely to lose its measles elimination status? The Pan American Health Organization commission that assesses measles and rubella elimination in the Americas was meeting Nov. 10-13 and expects to release its decision about the U.S. status on Nov. 17 . Many public health experts believe measles is once again endemic in the U.S. In early 2026, Maimuna Majumder, a computational epidemiologist at Boston Children’s Hospital, and other researchers analyzed the country’s measles data. Since then, Majumder said it “seems inevitable” that the U.S. would lose its elimination status. The U.S. is currently seeing uncontrolled measles spread that has lasted for more than 12 months, and available evidence signals that the actual number of infections surpasses the recorded case totals, Nuzzo said. "By every measure that matters, measles is no longer a rare condition in the United States," Nuzzo said. "It has returned to continual spread — spread that doesn’t require imported infections." 'We are deeply saddened': New York reports first measles death in more than 30 years Dr. Jonathan Temte, a University of Wisconsin public health and community engagement professor emeritus, said losing this designation — one the United States helped define years ago — says a lot about how the country’s public health and medical systems are doing. Measles elimination is a goal countries work toward, Nuzzo said: "It is a point of pride when you achieve it, and it is a point of embarrassment when you lose it." Could the U.S. eliminate measles again? Yes. Doing so starts with containing outbreaks. Canada lost its measles elimination designation in 2025, but still managed to control measles spread in 2026 — declaring its multi-jurisdictional measles outbreak over in October. Brazil lost its elimination status in 2019 and regained it in 2024. The U.S. was largely holding steady low case numbers from 2000 until 2019, when it recorded 1,274 cases. The 2018-19 outbreak was centered in New York City and surrounding counties, and officials responded by declaring a public health emergency, mandating vaccinations, closing schools with measles transmission, prohibiting unvaccinated children from attending school and doing public outreach, KFF reported . The New York outbreak was interrupted by August 2019 — less than 12 months after it began. It's important that sick people stay home and unvaccinated people who’ve been exposed undergo a 21-day quarantine , but widespread transmission only ends with increased vaccination, Nuzzo said. 'Measles-associated deaths': Dissecting the dispute between RFK Jr. and Pennsylvania's governor Noel Brewer, a public health professor at the University of North Carolina, presented the country’s measles report to the Pan American Health Organization commission. Vaccination is key, he said. "If we do not want an incurable and deadly disease to stalk our kids in schools, playgrounds and churches," Brewer said, adding, "We need to get more kids vaccinated." It's uncertain how public health officials will respond should the U.S. lose its status. In a Tuesday interview with Reuters , CDC Director Dr. Erica Schwartz said she didn’t foresee the federal government altering its approach. "We’re going to be doing the same exact thing that we’ve been doing today, which is working with the states," she said. PolitiFact Staff Researcher Caryn Baird contributed to this report. This article originally appeared on PolitiFact.com's Well Informed . It is republished here as part of a reporting and fact-checking partnership between PolitiFact and Hearst Television. Abortion Recedes as a Motivator for Democratic Voters Even As Abortion Rights Prevail in Most Ballot Initiatives Nov 13, 2026 Quick Insights An analysis of midterm voter survey data shows that abortion played a smaller role in voters’ decisions to turn out compared to past elections, but abortion rights continues to draw broad support in most states when put directly to voters in ballot initiatives. The Advancing Global Health Annual Program Statement (AGH/APS) Mechanism: Supplementing the America First MOUs Nov 13, 2026 Issue Brief To shed greater light on U.S. global health efforts under the AFGHS, this analysis examines the Advancing Global Health APS (AGH/APS) award opportunities announced to date by looking at how funding may be directed across countries and selected areas of global health programming, painting a fuller picture of how continued U.S. global health investment may look in a post-USAID foreign aid landscape. Measles Elimination Status: What It Is and How the U.S. Could Lose It Nov 13, 2026 Policy Watch This policy watch examines the United States’ measles elimination status, including what it means to eliminate measles, whether the current measles outbreak could threaten the country’s measles elimination status, and what losing that status might mean for measles control. A Step Back for U.S. Public Health: The U.S. Is Likely to Lose Its Measles Elimination Status Nov 13, 2026 Quick Insights The loss of measles elimination status would primarily carry symbolic weight, as the effect of rising cases and deaths is already clear. Still, it is a marker of how a combination of factors - COVID-19, increasing vaccine skepticism, and a growing partisan divide about vaccines - has been a step back for public health, and it is not clear when or if this trend will be reversed. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

经过数十年的进步,美国正与 麻疹 激增作斗争

U.S. struggles with measles surge after decades of progress
新闻简报麻疹 在美国卷土重来,自去年 1 月以来已报告数千例病例和 8 人死亡。暴发 主要集中在宾夕法尼亚州,但已扩展到 46 个州。这种死灰复燃的原因是人们对权威日益缺乏信任、建议混乱以及对授权的抵制。这种疾病引起了呼吸窘迫、严重的胃肠道症状,并给医疗保健系统,特别是急诊医学带来了额外的压力。由于该疾病的传染性很高,传播的风险很高。
查看新闻五要素
时间
我是威廉·布兰厄姆,这是《地平线》。麻疹,这是一种我们大多数人都没有记忆的疾病,尽管感染曾经是几乎每个美国儿童的现实。
地点与对象
美国 46 个州,重点是宾夕法尼亚州和纽约州。
事件
麻疹 在美国卷土重来,自去年 1 月以来已报告数千例病例和 8 人死亡。暴发 主要集中在宾夕法尼亚州,但已扩展到 46 个州。这种死灰复燃的原因是人们对权威日益缺乏信任、建议混乱以及对授权的抵制。
规模、影响与风险
自去年 1 月以来已有近 7,500 例病例,其中 8 人死亡。这种疾病引起了呼吸窘迫、严重的胃肠道症状,并给医疗保健系统,特别是急诊医学带来了额外的压力。由于该疾病的传染性很高,传播的风险很高。
应对、状态与不确定性
正在通过公共卫生运动和 疫苗接种 努力做出回应。然而,这种现象的死灰复燃是由多种因素造成的,包括对权威日益缺乏信任以及对授权的抵制,使得这种情况难以解决。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

U.S. struggles with measles surge after decades of progress

News Brief
Measles is making a resurgence in the U.S., with thousands of cases and eight deaths reported since January of last year. The outbreak is primarily centered in Pennsylvania but has spread to 46 states. The resurgence is attributed to a growing lack of trust in authority, confusing recommendations, and a pushback against mandates. The disease has caused respiratory distress, significant gastrointestinal symptoms, and put additional strains on the healthcare system, particularly in emergency medicine. The risk of spread is high due to the high infectivity rate of the illness.
Original source text (English)
I'm William Brangham and this is "Horizons." Measles, it's an illness most of us have no memory of, even though infection was once a reality for almost every American child. Today, this highly contagious virus is making a resurgence with thousands of people infected this year. The return of measles coming up next. ♪ Narrator: Support for "Horizons" has been provided by Steve and Marilyn Kerman and the Gordon and Betty Moore Foundation. Additional support is provided by Friends of the News Hour. ♪ This program was made possible by contributions to your PBS station from viewers like you. Thank you. From the David M. Rubenstein Studio at WETA in Washington, here is William Brangham. Welcome to "Horizons." When it comes to measles, the U.S. is going backwards. Our country, once a global leader in our ability to contain this virus, is now seeing measles surge back. Officially, there have been almost 7,500 cases since January of last year, but many believe that number is a vast undercount. Eight people have died. Some tight-knit religious communities in Pennsylvania were the epicenter of this current wave, but there have been outbreaks in 46 states this year. New York declared a state of emergency over its outbreak. Measles, of course, is prevented with a vaccine, the MMR vaccine, specifically, which protects against measles, mumps, and rubella. Over years of study, the MMR shot has proven to be a safe, effective, and lifesaving intervention. And while a majority of Americans still believe in the safety and effectiveness of vaccines, a small but growing number of them are turning away from those tools. Childhood vaccination rates in the U.S. are in decline. The number of families choosing non-medical vaccine exemptions at school is rising. So to understand how we got here and where we go now, we turn to two experts in public health. Dr. William Schaffner is a professor of preventive medicine at Vanderbilt University and also teaches at its medical school in the Department of Infectious Diseases. Jessica Malaty Rivera is a public health epidemiologist. She writes the wonderful Substack column, "'Making Science Makes Sense." Thank you both so much to you two for being here. I'm going to ask you both this question, starting with you, Dr. Schaffner, first. In 2000, we eliminated this virus from the country through a public health and vaccination campaign. And yet now we have thousands of cases across the country. Eight people have died. Help us understand, how did we get to this moment? Well, William, I think it took some time for us to get here. And it's been a combination of reasons. First, perhaps a growing lack of trust in people of authority, including those of us in the medical profession. Other things that contributed were confusing recommendations coming from professional societies, but also from at least previously respected federal institutions, such as the CDC. We also have today people who wish to do things in a more natural way. It's also true that we now have a couple of generations, the parents and the grandparents who've never seen measles. And if you don't respect measles, you won't value the vaccines. And so they ask questions, why? And then lastly, I would say, and this may be a consequence of what happened during COVID, there are some people who just push back against obligations, mandates. In most states, in fact, in all states, in order to attend daycare and school, unless you have a medical and in some places, religious or personal belief exemption, you have to be vaccinated against measles. And some people just push back against that compulsion. Jessica Malaty Rivera, what would you add to that? Like we have, again, we've just gone through this tidal shift from having this virus at bay to now having it all over the place. What would you add to that about how we've gotten into this mess? You know, one of the things that concerns me is this normalization of pediatric disease. There seems to be a trend that is okay with disease, disability, and even death of children when it comes to childhood diseases like measles. So much so that there have been people who say that measles infection is good for you, that it could prevent cancer, which it cannot, that it's mild or just a rash, and it is neither mild or just a rash. And so I do worry that we have become accustomed to preventable suffering among a young and vulnerable population because of a commitment to individual freedom. We've kind of lost our way when it comes to the reasons why we vaccinate for viruses like measles, where we don't just do it to protect ourselves, we do it to protect those who cannot be vaccinated, which includes infants. Four of the eight deaths that have been reported this year have been among infants who were too young to be vaccinated. Those infants rely on their communities to be vaccinated so that measles doesn't spread. And I think that we've lost our way with that. I'd like to play a little bit of an interview that my colleague and I did with a doctor from Lancaster, Pennsylvania, she works in an emergency room there, about what they have been seeing in their community. This is Dr. Elizabeth Werley. Let's listen to what she told us. I'm Elizabeth Werley and I am the medical director of the emergency department at Penn State Health Lancaster Medical Center. And we are in the heart of the measles outbreak here in Lancaster County in Pennsylvania. For many of us in practice, we're only familiar with it from a textbook. So to actually witness and see what our patients are experiencing, they're in respiratory distress, they're having trouble breathing, they have significant GI symptoms. I do believe that lack of immunization is a factor in the current measles outbreak. We do have a large plain community here in Lancaster County and in Central Pennsylvania, but we also know it's out in the community, the general community as well. And even when other patients are immunized, we still have to worry about those patients that are at high risk, whether it is neonates who can't be immunized or those patients with other conditions or medications that put their immune system at risk. We have decades of information, both in our country and from other countries, demonstrating that the vaccinations are safe and they are effective. What I would tell someone if they're concerned about a measles exposure is to either get immunized if they have not been immunized, or they can have blood work with their primary care doctor to check their titers to see if their immunization still is active. And if they are experiencing symptoms that they are concerned about, that they should isolate at home and not go out into the community because of the high infectivity rate of the illness. It's very easy to spread. It can stay in the environment for a few hours afterwards. We have to take care of our community in addition to the patients that we're taking care of here in the hospital. I mean, it does put additional strains on an overworked healthcare system, and especially in emergency medicine, it's just one more thing that we have to be aware of to keep us on our toes. Dr. Schaffner, listening to her talk, there's so many factors in there. One of them that she was stressing is the contagiousness, the infectivity of this virus. For people who don't appreciate just how powerful this virus is, can you just remind us, how does it compare, say, to how easy it is to catch COVID or a cold? William, it is the most contagious virus that we know. One of the things she alluded to was a person who's shedding, breathes out the measles virus, can leave the room, and if a susceptible person comes into that room up to two hours later, they can still inhale the few viral particles that are hovering in the air and become infected and become ill. This is a virus that is extremely contagious, and obviously, that's why we want to vaccinate everyone. As Ms. Rivera said, we're protecting not only ourselves, our own children, our own families, but we're contributing to protection of everyone in the neighborhood and in our communities, because there are little babies who could get measles, and they have high mortality associated with that, who can't be vaccinated yet, and there are people who are immune-compromised. They can't take the vaccine. How do we protect them? All the rest of us are protected. We create a cocoon of protection around them so that the virus can't get to those most susceptible who live amongst us, our more frail brothers and sisters. Jessica, I mentioned that the official count, quote-unquote, "is 7,500 cases nationally this year." Do you think that that's an accurate picture of how widespread measles is in the country right now? I do not. I think that is a massive undercount, and I say that with confidence because if we've already seen eight deaths reported this year, which is a tragic milestone that we just hit this week, typically what you would see in a measles outbreak is one to three deaths per 1, 000 confirmed cases, so if you do a little bit of rough math, that would mean we would be well over 8000 cases to see a death rate at eight so far, and there's a few reasons for that. There is a slow-to-report sentiment in a lot of these closed communities. It is also just so massive that contact tracing and reporting is very overwhelming for departments of health. You have to recognize the fact that a measles outbreak is extremely resource-expensive for departments of health, both in the time and the personnel and even the cost of managing that size of an outbreak, and so I think it is much, much bigger than what we see right now. Dr. Schaffner, I'm curious, if someone is hearing all of this, worried about perhaps this very big undercount of measles, if they get vaccinated, how much protection does that offer you? Is this a bulletproof, 100% protection? How much does it give you? It's close to bulletproof. It's among the best vaccines we have. If you take the two recommended doses, you will get somewhere between 97% and 98% for likelihood of protection for the rest of your life, and even if you do happen to get a breakthrough infection, namely you're vaccinated, you're exposed, and you still get an infection, your illness will be milder, so this is a terrific vaccine in terms of effectiveness and it's also terrifically safe. There are very few adverse events associated with this vaccine. It's been used in the literal billions of doses around the world for 50 years now. Jessica Malaty Rivera, HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. plays a complicated role here. At times, when asked about measles outbreaks, he has said, "Yes, go get vaccinated, "it will protect you," but in his previous career and in his current role in the federal government, he has done an enormous amount to sow distrust about vaccines and say that they are dangerous, they are harming people, that the government is not being square with people about this. How much responsibility do you place at the leadership of the federal government right now? I think he and the federal government bear a huge amount of responsibility because I think that this movement or this unprecedented outbreak didn't necessarily start at the beginning of it. There were seeds that were planted years and years ago by RFK Jr. himself and by people from his organization, his former organization, Children's Health Defense, and many like both of those actors who were sowing seeds of doubt about the MMR vaccine safety, the MMR vaccine's effectiveness, even the MMR vaccine's utility. And I think he would even say today that the only way to prevent the virus, the infection, is through the MMR vaccine. But what he's also saying is that it's okay to get it and it's okay to suffer for it because it's just a mild disease. And this is where I think that the normalization of disease has become so ubiquitous among people who are anti-MMR vaccine because they've been sold a lie. They've been sold a lie that it is something that can be treated with vitamin A and it cannot, that it's something that is typical for childhood when, you know, I'm grateful that I live in a time where my children don't have to experience a disease that was so prevalent back in the 80s. Even varicella is an example of that, that we have the tools to prevent them. Suffering can be avoided. It should not be the expectation simply because there are ways in which you could care for somebody. Now, it's also important to note there is no known, like, specific treatment for measles. It's all supportive care. And some kids will get pneumonia, and pneumonia is the number one cause of measles-related death. Some kids may get encephalitis. That is a very dangerous swelling of the brain that can cause a lot of permanent damage, including hearing loss and blindness and other impairments. It can cause complications years after recovery. And a rare encephalitis called SSPE can cause immune amnesia. These are not benign complications of a disease that is almost nearly entirely preventable through two doses of that vaccine. And RFK Jr. absolutely knows this, and it is the responsibility of the administration and our HHS secretary to make it extremely clear that kids do not have to be getting a very preventable disease. Dr. Schaffner, RFK Jr. has also suggested that vaccines, including MMR, cause a great deal of injury to individuals and that the government has not really done a good job of studying this. He's actually, under his leadership, set up a panel at the NIH to study vaccine injury in people. Is there any evidence of that? I mean, you mentioned before the safety record of this vaccine, but is there any evidence that the government knows that this is doing more damage to people and not telling us, or that these vaccines are complicated in any way? Well, William, before a vaccine is licensed, it is studied very, very carefully in large populations, not only how well it works, but whether it's safe. And then, more importantly, on an ongoing basis, after the vaccine is used, its safety continues to be monitored very carefully, not just by us, but by health departments, ministries of health around the world. And there is a uniformity of agreement that these vaccines are exceedingly safe. Adverse events associated with them, no vaccine, no pharmaceutical is perfect. They do occur rarely. And we have a compensation program that has been established in the United States to recognize those rare adverse events and provide at least some financial compensation to the families involved. So this has been thought about and studied with enormous care. If you look at the standard vaccine textbooks under measles vaccine, they have a whole large section under safety describing all of these studies that have been done. Brangham: Jessica Malaty Rivera, again, I can't help but know that we've got this ocean of misinformation out there. I just saw a study in JAMA this week that a survey of over 30,000 people and one in four Americans think that vaccines or taking Tylenol when you're pregnant is a major risk factor for autism. How do we...? As you write, making science makes sense. You are a science communicator. You're trying to help people understand the efficacy and safety of these. How do we go about combating this? It is so challenging. I can't deny the fact that this is incredibly difficult work, especially because even if the data are on our side, even if it was debunked, even if the paper that originally made the claim that connecting vaccines and specifically the MMR vaccine to autism was retracted, even if that person who wrote the paper had their medical license retracted as well or rescinded, it doesn't change the fact that something like that is incredibly sticky. And there's a lot of literature about the psychology and the dynamics of misinformation and why they stick, why they continue to spread and grow legs and continue to kind of morph into people's worldviews about public health. This is one of those things that has unfortunately remained very, very strong in people's minds when it comes to vaccine safety. And I think it is partially because it evokes a lot of fear. A lot of the misinformation that we see based on vaccines is intended to cause a very intense emotional reaction. And it dysregulates people. And dysregulated people consuming more misinformation just causes an information ecosystem that becomes even more difficult for trained and untrained eyes to decipher what is true and what is not true. We're dealing with people who are outside of their expertise opining on issues about vaccinology, about immunology, about infectious diseases, but said with conviction, said with identifying features that seem connected to the audience, whether that be a person who's a parent, whether that be a person who's an advocate, those types of things kind of suspend the facts, unfortunately. So even if the data on our side, and I'll kind of reiterate too, that there are... I can't think of a vaccine that is more scrutinized than the MMR vaccine, other than the COVID-19 vaccine, in which we've specifically looked into it to see if it has any credibility, this claim about MMR and autism. They've done massive studies comparing siblings, comparing twins, comparing people from across populations in different vaccine schedules, simply to say, "Can we confidently prove "that there is an association or even a link?" And we have not been able to do that. And so unfortunately, emotions and anecdotes outweigh data in situations like this, which is why storytelling and empathy in your science communication have to be part of your strategy, because at this point, you're not dealing with facts against facts. I'm going to do a couple of all of that. Just to expand on that, if I may just quickly, we tend to give our patients facts that goes to your brain, but the behavioral psychologists have told us, just as Jessica has mentioned, that how we...

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

麻疹:它是什么、它如何传播以及如果您怀疑它该怎么办 - EMS1

Measles: What it is, how it spreads and what to do if you suspect it - EMS1
新闻简报本文来自EMS1,于2026年11月09日发布,讨论了在美国麻疹的再次兴起,特别是在近年来未充分免疫的社区中突出暴发。文章指出,麻疹曾在2000年被宣布在美国根除,但近年来出现了显著的反弹,例如2014年迪士尼乐园的暴发暴发和2019年太平洋西北地区和纽约市的暴发。文章概述了麻疹感染的阶段、症状和预防策略,包括MMR 疫苗,该疫苗在两剂接种时有效率高达99%。文章还提供了应急响应人员处理潜在麻疹病例的5步指南,强调了个人防护装备、症状评估和隔离程序的重要性。CDC建议在12至15个月时接种MMR 疫苗的第一剂,在4至6岁时接种第二剂。家长应留意严重的症状,如高烧、呼吸困难或神经系统问题,并在必要时立即寻求医疗护理。文章未报告当前暴发的具体病例数或死亡人数,但强调了麻疹的历史影响,估计每年有500人死亡和48,000人住院,直到1963年MMR 疫苗疫苗的引入。信息基于CDC和约翰霍普金斯大学,文章旨在提高公众意识和应急响应指导。
查看新闻五要素
时间
文章发表于2026年11月09日。麻疹 暴发描述为近年来发生,特别是在未充分接种疫苗的社区中,特别是在2014年和2019年出现显著增加。
地点与对象
文章重点关注美国,特别提到了免疫不足社区的 暴发,包括太平洋西北地区和纽约市的 2014 年迪士尼乐园 暴发 和 2019 年 暴发。
事件
本文概述了 麻疹、其症状和预防策略。它还包括基于 CDC 和约翰·霍普金斯大学信息的紧急响应人员处理潜在 麻疹 病例的 5 步骤指南。
规模、影响与风险
Prior to the measles vaccine, the U.S. saw an estimated 3-4 million cases annually, resulting in about 500 deaths, 48,000 hospitalizations, and 1,000 cases of encephalitis each year. The 2019 outbreaks resulted in the highest number of cases since 1992. Measles can be fatal in rare cases, with one to three of every 1,000 infected children dying.
应对、状态与不确定性
CDC 建议将 疫苗接种 与 MMR 疫苗 结合使用,两种剂量同时服用时,有效率达 99%。建议应急响应人员遵循处理潜在 麻疹 病例的 5 步指南,包括个人防护装备要求、症状评估、现场管理、支持性护理指导和事件后净化。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles: What it is, how it spreads and what to do if you suspect it - EMS1

News Brief
This article from EMS1, published on November 09, 2026, discusses the resurgence of measles in the United States, highlighting outbreaks in recent years, particularly in under-immunized communities. It notes that measles was declared eliminated in the U.S. in 2000 but has seen notable surges, such as the 2014 Disneyland outbreak and the 2019 outbreaks in the Pacific Northwest and New York City. The article outlines the stages of measles infection, its symptoms, and prevention strategies, including the MMR vaccine, which is 99% effective when both doses are given. It also provides a 5-step guide for emergency responders handling potential measles cases, emphasizing the importance of PPE, symptoms assessment, and isolation protocols. The CDC recommends the first dose of the MMR vaccine at 12–15 months and the second at 4–6 years. Parents are advised to watch for severe symptoms such as high fever, difficulty breathing, or neurological issues and seek immediate medical care if necessary. The article does not report specific case counts or deaths for the current outbreak but highlights the historical impact of measles, with an estimated 500 deaths and 48,000 hospitalizations annually before the vaccine's introduction in 1963. The information is based on the CDC and Johns Hopkins University, and the article serves as a guide for public awareness and emergency response.

to do if you suspect it Think you or someone you know might have measles? Here’s what you need to know November 09, 2026 03:44 PM • Rachel Engel Measles: What you need to know Video Player is loading. Measles, a highly contagious viral disease, is on the rise again in the United States. While measles was declared to be eradicated from the U.S. in 2000, outbreaks continue to occur, primarily due to declining immunization coverage in some communities. In this article, you’ll find an overview of the disease, symptoms and prevention strategies. We also include a 5-step guide for emergency responders handling potential measles cases, including: PPE requirements Symptoms assessment Scene management Supportive care guidance Post-incident decontamination If you think you or someone you know has contracted measles, or you believe you may have come into contact with someone infected by the virus, read on to learn what to look for and when to seek medical help. What is measles? Measles has been a known disease for centuries, with cases documented as early as the 9th century, according to the CDC. In the U.S., measles was once a common childhood illness, with nearly every child contracting the virus before the age of 15. Prior to the introduction of the measles vaccine in 1963, the country saw an estimated 3-4 million cases annually, resulting in about 500 deaths, 48,000 hospitalizations and 1,000 cases of encephalitis each year. The widespread use of the measles-mumps-rubella (MMR) vaccine led to a dramatic decline in cases. By 2000, measles was declared eliminated in the U.S., meaning there was no continuous transmission of the disease for over a year. However, due to global travel and vaccine hesitancy, outbreaks have occurred in recent years, particularly in under-immunized communities. Notable surges include the 2014 outbreak linked to Disneyland and the 2019 outbreaks in the Pacific Northwest and New York City, which resulted in the highest number of cases since 1992. What does measles look like? Because the virus progresses in stages, with each stage presenting different physical symptoms, you can estimate how long until the illness has run its course based on how the infection is currently presenting. Get an idea as to how measles symptoms present on different areas of the body below: What are the symptoms of measles? Measles symptoms typically appear several days after infection and progress through several stages: STAGE 1 — Infection and incubation: Lasting 10-14 days, there are no signs or symptoms as the measles virus spreads through the body. STAGE 2 — Mild initial onset: The first measles symptoms mimic a mild cold, with a moderate fever, runny nose, cough and sore throat being the most common symptoms. This stage lasts 2-3 days, and is also accompanied by Koplik’s spots — tiny white spots surrounded by a red background found on the inner lining of the cheek inside the mouth. STAGE 3 — Rash and high fever: A red, blotchy rash emerges around the hairline and face before spreading downward to the chest, arms, legs and feet. As the rash spreads, the fever rises, reaching as high as 105°F. This stage typically lasts 6-7 days. STAGE 4 — Recovery: When the measles rash recedes, so does the high fever. The rash will disappear in the same way it appered, beginning with the face and working down the body. This stage may last up to 10 days, with the couch lingering and the rash darkening and/or peeling as it disappears. What should parents should watch for in kids infected with measles? Parents should be vigilant for: High fever that persists for several days Worsening cough or difficulty breathing Severe dehydration (dry mouth, lack of urination, sunken eyes) Signs of complications such as ear infections, pneumonia or encephalitis (seizures, confusion, or lethargy) Rash progression and persistence If a child exhibits difficulty breathing, persistent high fever or neurological symptoms, parents should seek emergency medical care immediately. Is measles deadly? Yes, in very rare cases, measles can be fatal. According to Johns Hopkins University, “measles kills between one and three of every 1,000 infected children.” How is measles spread? Measles is highly contagious and is spread through respiratory droplets when an infected person coughs, sneezes or talks. The virus can remain airborne for up to an hour in a confined space. Prevention strategies include: Vaccination: The MMR vaccine is 99% effective at preventing measles when both doses are administered. The CDC recommends children receive the first dose at 12–15 months and the second dose at 4–6 years. Isolation: Anyone infected should stay home for at least 4 days after the rash appears and avoid public places. Good hygiene: Covering coughs and sneezes, handwashing and disinfecting surfaces can help limit virus spread. As an adult, if I had measles as a child, should I get the vaccine? If you had measles as a child, you do not need a measles vaccine. Your body has already built up an immune defense to the virus and you are not at risk of being infected again. Most people born before 1957 are considered to have “presumptive evidence” of immunity, due to the virus being so widespread prior to the vaccine become available, that nearly everyone had been infected at some point. If I was vaccinated for measles, do I need a booster? If you received one dose of the MMR vaccine as a child, your immunity is estimated at 97% against viral infection, and 99% if you received two doses. This is considered to provide lifetime immunity, and no booster shots are necessary. 5 steps for EMS response to measles If your agency is called to respond to a suspected case of measles, take adequate precautions to protect both the patient and yourself. 1. Don PPE in accordance with your agency’s policy Measles is highly contagious and spreads via airborne droplets, so proper PPE is critical to minimize exposure. Before approaching the patient, responders may be required to wear an N95 respirator, gloves, eye protection or gown if available. 2. Assess the patient for measles symptoms Look for classic measles symptoms, which include: High fever (often over 101°F) The “3 Cs": Cough, coryza (runny nose), conjunctivitis Koplik spots Red, blotchy rash starting on the face and spreading downward Gather travel history or exposure risk, especially if the patient has been in contact with someone with measles or has recently traveled to areas with outbreaks. 3. Isolate the patient from vulnerable individuals Because the virus is so easily transmitted, if measles is suspected, isolate the patient from anyone on scene who is not immune or is vulnerable to infection. If transporting in an ambulance, ensure negative pressure ventilation (if available) or open windows for airflow, and limit the number of responders who have direct contact with the patient. 4. Provide supportive care As there is no specific antiviral treatment for measles, care is supportive: Hydration: Encourage fluids if the patient can drink. Fever management: Use cool compresses; avoid aspirin in children (risk of Reye’s syndrome). Monitor airway and breathing: If respiratory distress or hypoxia occurs, provide oxygen therapy as needed. Avoid pharmaceuticals: Do not administer steroids or antibiotics unless there is a secondary bacterial infection. 5. Decon equipment and notify public health officials Measles virus can remain airborne for up to 2 hours after an infected person leaves the area. After patient care: Disinfect all equipment and surfaces with appropriate hospital-grade disinfectants. Report the case to local health authorities immediately. The future of measles in the U.S. Measles is a preventable but serious disease that can cause severe complications, especially in young children and immunocompromised individuals. While the U.S. has made significant progress in measles control, outbreaks remain a threat due to declining vaccination rates. Parents, healthcare professionals and emergency responders must remain vigilant in recognizing symptoms, preventing transmission and ensuring high immunization coverage to protect public health. Additional resources: CDC measles guidance Weekly update of U.S. measles cases This article, originally published in March 2025, has been updated. Measles cases pass 300 in Ohio as US is poised to lose elimination status - WOSU Public Media

News Brief
Ohio has reported 310 confirmed measles cases, with 300 cases in the state as of November 10, 2026. The US is at risk of losing its measles elimination status, with 5,182 cases nationwide. Public health officials recommend vaccination, particularly two doses of the MMR vaccine, which provides 97% protection. Complications include pneumonia, encephalitis, and subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE).

Facilities & Rentals WOSU Events Calendar Facilities & Rentals Airfare Health, Science & Environment Measles cases pass 300 in Ohio as US is poised to lose elimination status Ideastream Public Media | By Ida Lieszkovszky Published November 10, 2026 at 11:12 AM EST Facebook Threads Twitter LinkedIn Email Leigh Prather / Adobe Stock Images According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, two doses of the MMR vaccine are 97% effective at preventing measles. There have been 310 confirmed cases of measles in Ohio since January, as the measles epidemic continues to spread nationwide. Ashtabula, Geauga and Wayne counties account for roughly half of all cases statewide. As of Nov. 5, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention has confirmed 5,182 cases across the country. The spike in measles comes as the Pan American Health Organization convenes a meeting to determine the measles and rubella elimination status of the Americas. The U.S. is at risk of losing its measles elimination status. Losing elimination status happens when a country cannot stop the spread of measles over a 12-month period. Put another way, measles is considered endemic in a country if there is continuous transmission over a 12-month period. The U.S. has been dealing with a series of measles outbreaks since early 2025. Canada lost its measles elimination status last year. The best thing people can do to protect themselves from measles is to make sure they’re fully vaccinated, according to Dr. Claudia Hoyen, the director of pediatric infection control at University Hospitals’ Rainbow Babies Children’s. “Measles is not just a rash. There can be pretty significant complications,” she said. “One in 20 kids will end up with pneumonia and potentially in the hospital. We worry terribly about complications like encephalitis, which you can get if you have the measles.” Encephalitis is an inflammation of the brain tissue. Measles can also lead to dangerous long-term complications, including subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), which is a rare but fatal degenerative disease. SSPE can take between seven and 15 years to emerge after an initial measles infection and is particularly dangerous when children catch measles at a young age, especially under 2. It is not caused by the MMR vaccine. Ohio Department of Health Geauga county has recorded 54 cases of measles in 2026, and Ashtabula another 47. The MMR vaccine is typically given in two doses during childhood. Hoyen said older adults can check with their doctors if they’re concerned about their immunity level. “I think the best thing that people can do is understand what their immunity is,” she said. "Once you have that immunity then that's a good thing because it's going to keep you protected.” Two doses of the MMR vaccine leads to 97% protection against measles. According to the CDC, 96% of the measles cases nationwide are in people who are either unvaccinated or whose vaccine status is unknown. Hoyen said there are interventions that can help minimize the complications associated with measles in unvaccinated individuals. Getting the MMR vaccine within the first three days of contracting measles can help prevent infection or reduce the severity of the illness. She said for people who are immunocompromised or pregnant, there are immunoglobulin treatments available to help protect against complications. Experts are asking people who think they may have been exposed to the measles virus or are experiencing symptoms associated with measles to call ahead to their local hospital rather than just walking into a doctor’s office or the emergency room. That’s because the measles virus can linger in the air or on surfaces for up to two hours after an infected individual has left a space. It can take seven to 14 days for symptoms to emerge after an initial exposure. Measles symptoms include a high fever, cough, runny nose and watery eyes. It’s also typically accompanied by white spots that can appear inside the mouth and a rash that starts on the face and spreads downward on the body. There have been several local warnings of potential measles exposures in Northeast Ohio. Akron Children’s Hospital warned the public of a possible measles exposure at the Golisano Campus on Oct. 20. Health officials also warned of a possible exposure at SweetBerry Fresh Market in Wickliffe on Oct. 3, 5, or 6. In Warren, officials warned of a possible exposure at Mercy Health St. Joseph Warren Hospital’s emergency room on Aug. 23. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

威斯康星州国土安全部报告与 麻疹 暴发 有关的第二例死亡

Wisconsin DHS reports second death linked to measles outbreak
新闻简报威斯康星州卫生服务部 (DHS) 报告了格兰特县出现第二例与 麻疹 暴发 有关的死亡,自 2026 年 8 月以来,20 个县已有 649 例死亡病例,这是 35 年来的最高数字。暴发 存在危及生命的并发症的风险,DHS 正在努力协调和支持应对措施,提供近 2,000 剂 MMR 疫苗 并管理超过 45,600 剂。预防 麻疹 的最佳方法是使用 MMR 疫苗,两次剂量的有效率约为 97%。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026-11-13T14:07:00Z - 2026-11-13T14:07:00Z.
地点与对象
美国威斯康星州格兰特县。
事件
已确认与 麻疹 暴发 有关的第二例死亡。
规模、影响与风险
自2026年8月以来,20个县有649例麻疹病例,为35年来最高数量,存在危及生命的并发症风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
DHS 继续与地方和部落卫生部门合作,协调和支持全州范围内的应对措施,已交付近 2,000 剂 MMR 疫苗,已注射超过 45,600 剂。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Wisconsin DHS reports second death linked to measles outbreak

News Brief
Wisconsin Department of Health Services (DHS) reported a second death linked to a measles outbreak in Grant County, with 649 cases in 20 counties since August 2026, the highest number in over 35 years. The outbreak poses a risk of life-threatening complications, and DHS is working to coordinate and support the response, delivering nearly 2,000 doses of MMR vaccine and administering over 45,600 doses. The best way to prevent measles is with the MMR vaccine, which is approximately 97% effective with two doses.
Original source text (English)
Skip to content Small Towns3 Brilliant Minutes4:30 In-Depth2 Your HealthAsk The ExpertAllergy TrackNextGen TVBucks News Watch Live Weather Sports Consumer Alert Traffic Links Homepage WBAY History News Action 2 News This Morning LIVESTREAM Community Crime Health High School National Politics Small Towns State Wisconsin 360 Videos Live Newscasts Breaking News Video Weather Weather Alerts Closings FirstAlert Weather 24/7 Pinpoint Digital Doppler Map Room Allergy Track SkyCam Network Severe Weather Safety Aches & Pains Forecast Get Our Weather App Get a Weather Radio Tornado Coverage Sports Scoreboard Packers Operation Football National Milwaukee Bucks Traffic Contact Us 2 Your Health Ask The Expert Birthday Club Circle Country Community Calendar Consumer Alert Featured Links Local News Live InvestigateTV Watching Your Wallet Investigation Lottery Meet the Team PowerNation Recalls Safety Desk Submit a Tip Submit Photos and Videos WBAY Jobs Snow & Ski Report NextGen TV WBAY Marketing What's on TV Digital Marketing Gray AI Policy BREAKING: The Federal Aviation Administration says airplane departures can resume Wisconsin DHS reports second death linked to measles outbreak The death of a child was reported in Grant County Measles(WNEM) By WBAY news staff Published: Nov. 13, 2026 at 2:07 PM CST|Updated: 6 hours ago Share Add Us On GoogleAdd as a preferred source on Google GRANT COUNTY, Wis. (WBAY) - The Wisconsin Department of Health Services and the Grant County Health Department confirmed a pediatric measles-associated death in Wisconsin, the second death connected to the state’s measles outbreak in 2026. To protect the privacy of the family and the child, DHS said it will not release any additional information about the death. Case counts As of Nov. 13, 2026, DHS has reported 649 measles cases in 20 counties since August 2026. DHS said this is the highest number of cases in the state in more than 35 years. Reported case counts may not reflect the full extent of measles spread. Not everyone who becomes ill seeks medical care or gets tested, which means some cases may go unreported. About measles Measles is a highly contagious virus that spreads through the air and through close contact. It can cause life-threatening complications, including pneumonia and swelling of the brain. Anyone can become seriously ill. Children younger than 5, pregnant people and people with weakened immune systems are at increased risk of complications. State response DHS continues to work with local and Tribal health departments and health care partners to coordinate and support the statewide response. The efforts include supporting case identification and tracking, information sharing, testing and vaccination. Since Aug. 1, 2026, DHS has delivered nearly 2,000 doses of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine to local health departments in communities with confirmed cases, and it is making additional doses available. Over 45,600 total doses of MMR vaccine have been administered by local public health agencies and health care providers in Wisconsin during this time. “Preventing the spread of measles takes action from all of us,” said State Health Officer and Division of Public Health Administrator Paula Tran. “From making sure our families are protected by getting the recommended vaccinations to staying home when we may have been exposed, we encourage everyone to work together to protect those who are most vulnerable to serious illness. Stay informed, look out for one another, and take the actions you can to help stop the spread of measles and keep our communities healthy and safe.” Vaccination The best way to prevent measles is with the MMR vaccine. Two doses are approximately 97% effective at preventing measles. Wisconsin residents can check their vaccination records online using the Wisconsin Immunization Registry. They can also contact their health care provider or local health department. If you are exposed DHS advises anyone who has been exposed to measles to stay home. People who develop a fever and rash, especially if they have been exposed to measles or are not vaccinated, should contact their health care provider, DHS said. They should call ahead before visiting a clinic or hospital so steps can be taken to prevent exposing others. Copyright 2026 WBAY. All rights reserved. 一个人可能在俄亥俄州的六个地点传播了 麻疹 - Akron Beacon Journal

One person may have spread measles at a half-dozen sites in Ohio - Akron Beacon Journal
新闻简报俄亥俄州 Geauga 县和 Lake 县的卫生官员正在调查一例确诊的 麻疹 病例,该病例可能在多个地点(包括学校和餐馆)暴露了多人。该病例正在调查中,卫生官员正在敦促 麻疹,因为今年俄亥俄州的 麻疹 病例数量与 2025 年相比增加了近 600%。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026-11-12 12:07 下午美国东部时间 - 2026 年 11 月 12 日 12:07
地点与对象
美国俄亥俄州东北部的格奥加县和莱克县。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

One person may have spread measles at a half-dozen sites in Ohio - Akron Beacon Journal

News Brief
Health officials in Geauga and Lake counties, Ohio, are investigating a confirmed measles case that may have exposed multiple individuals at several locations, including schools and restaurants. The case is under investigation, and health officials are urging vaccination, as the number of measles cases in Ohio has increased by nearly 600% this year compared to 2025.
Original source text (English)
FAA says it has lifted nationwide ground stop that snarled airports Friday News Sports Entertainment Lifestyle Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTHCARE One person may have spread measles at a half-dozen sites in Ohio Chad Murphy USA TODAY NETWORK Updated Nov. 12, 2026, 12:07 p.m. ET A confirmed measles case may have led to multiple being exposed at several locations in Northeast Ohio. Health officials have released a list of potential exposure sites in Geauga and Lake counties, including two schools and two restaurants. Ohio has seen a nearly 600% increase in measles cases this year compared to all of last year. The vast majority of measles cases in Ohio are among individuals who are not vaccinated. The measles vaccine is 97% effective, and vaccination is the best way to prevent the disease. One person infected with measles may have spread the highly contagious disease over a period of about a week at more than a half-dozen sites in Northeast Ohio. Geauga Public Health is reporting a confirmed case of measles with exposures at several public locations in Geauga and Lake counties during the time period the individual was able to spread the virus. Statewide, the Ohio Department of Health is reporting 310 cases in 25 counties so far this year, according to its most recent summary of infectious diseases dashboard update on Nov. 5. That number is nearly six times (588.9%) more than the 45 cases reported in all of 2025. The vast majority of cases in Ohio are among unvaccinated individuals, per ODH. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Here's where measles may have spread in Geauga and Lake counties. Here's where people may have been exposed to the measles virus in Lake and Geauga counties According to Geauga Public Health, people may have been exposed to measles during these dates and times, and at these locations: Monday Nov. 2 to Thursday Nov. 5, from about 7:45 a.m. to 5:15 p.m. Berkshire Middle School, 14155 Claridon Troy Road, Burton, Ohio Tuesday Nov. 3, from about 3:30 to 7 p.m. Kent State University - Geauga Campus, 14111 Claridon Troy Road, Burton, Ohio Tuesday, Nov. 3 and Thursday Nov. 5, during evening (dinner) hours The Hambone, 13960 Rock Creek Road, Chardon, Ohio Tuesday Nov. 3, from about 5:30 to 9:30 p.m. Berkshire Local Schools Fall Choir Concert, 14155 Claridon Troy Road, Burton, Ohio Friday, Nov. 6, from 10 a.m. to 2 p.m. SPC Sports, 9431 Mentor Ave., Mentor, Ohio Friday, Nov. 6, from about 11 a.m. to 4 p.m. Longhorn Steakhouse, 9557 Mentor Ave., Mentor, Ohio Saturday, Nov. 7, from 5:54 to 8:48 p.m. University Hospitals Urgent Care Concord, 7500 Auburn Road, Suite 1200, Concord, Ohio Can you get measles if you have been vaccinated? Two doses of measles, mumps and rubella vaccine are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but it can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before through four days after a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. Gordon County, Catoosa County, Carroll County, Haralson County, Bartow County, Cherokee County, Dade County, Pickens County, Walker County, Fannin County Why Georgia measles cases are surging past last year's totals By FOX 5 Atlanta Digital Team FOX 5 Atlanta Georgia Published November 10, 2026 8:54 PM EST Share Copy Link Email Facebook Twitter LinkedIn Reddit Published November 10, 2026 8:54 PM EST The Brief State health officials confirmed three new measles cases in Macon County linked to an earlier October exposure. The latest infections raise Georgia's total count to 28 cases, nearly tripling the state's tally recorded throughout 2025. Health leaders urge anyone displaying measles symptoms to contact medical providers by telephone before visiting clinics or emergency facilities. MACON, Ga. - Three new measles cases have been confirmed in Macon County among people exposed to a previous patient, state health officials announced Tuesday. Macon County measles outbreak What we know: The Georgia Department of Public Health confirmed the three individuals contracted the virus after coming into close contact with a person diagnosed on Oct. 12. Agency staff members have notified other community members who may have faced exposure and carry an elevated risk of falling ill. Dermatologists Beg Women With Thin Hair To Use This Household ItemTop Austin Hairstylist | SponsoredSponsored Learn More Undo The three diagnoses bring the total count across Georgia to 28 confirmed cases, according to state health records. That figure marks a sharp climb from 2025, which saw 10 cases reported statewide during the entire year. Preventing measles virus exposure What we don't know: Health authorities have not identified the specific public locations where possible community exposures occurred or the current condition of the three patients. Officials also have not released the ages or vaccination status of the individuals who tested positive. The Georgia Department of Public Health advises anyone experiencing symptoms to call a doctor's office, hospital, or public health clinic ahead of time rather than arriving unannounced. Calling in advance prevents the virus from spreading through healthcare waiting areas. Measles in Georgia Timeline: Georgia has experienced a sharp uptick in measles cases since late summer, jumping from seven confirmed infections in mid-August to 25 by late October, according to the Georgia Department of Public Health. That total more than doubles the 10 infections recorded across the state in all of 2025. The surge began on Aug. 18 when state health officials confirmed that an unvaccinated metro Atlanta resident tested positive with no reported travel history or known exposure. Within days, transmission spread into a childcare center, leading to four confirmed pediatric cases on Aug. 28 and three additional cases on Sept. 4 linked to the daycare cluster. Outbreaks widened beyond the metro area in the weeks that followed. On Sept. 30, state epidemiologists verified that five siblings in Jasper County contracted the virus following out-of-state travel. By mid-October, cases emerged in central Georgia when an unvaccinated Macon County resident tested positive following exposure to a visitor linked to a broader outbreak in Pennsylvania. The infection triggered a public health exposure alert for patrons of a Perry steakhouse on Oct. 19, followed on Oct. 27 by three additional positive tests among the resident's close contacts. State public health officials noted that nearly all of the confirmed patients were unvaccinated or not fully vaccinated against the airborne virus. Protecting families through vaccination Dig deeper: Medical professionals emphasize that immunization remains the most reliable defense against the disease. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention recommends children receive two doses of the MMR vaccine, targeting measles, mumps and rubella. Under federal guidelines, pediatricians administer the first shot between 12 and 15 months of age, followed by a second dose between ages 4 and 6. Parents seeking information about immunization schedules can consult their personal healthcare providers or visit public health portals provided by state and federal health agencies. The Source: The information in this story was gathered from a news release issued by the Georgia Department of Public Health and guidance from the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Doctor Urges Anyone Over 60 With Constipation to Drink 1 Cup of This News/Commentary Babies are getting measles in the womb. That wasn’t a problem before the vaccine, pediatricians say November 11, 2026 | Libby Bandelin | Print Article Share Tweet Truth Gettr Telegram A blue state recently reported an alarming rise of infants born with measles and pushed vaccination — these cases may be the result of lost natural immunization after the introduction of the MMR vaccine, doctors say. The Pennsylvania Department of Health (DOH) issued an alert about an increase in measles cases during pregnancy on Oct. 19, and confirmed that 66 mothers had come down with the disease and at least six babies were born with it in Pennsylvania throughout 2026, doctors told NBC. While the DOH said that day in a press release that getting the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine “is the best way to prevent measles and newborn measles,” some pediatricians and family practice doctors disagree, according to an article published Monday in the Defender, Children’s Health Defense‘s news source. Doctors who spoke with the outlet said that congenital measles — when a baby is born with the disease passed down by his or her mother in utero — used to be a rare occurrence before the vaccine was introduced more than 50 years ago. They added that decades of research shows that vaccine-induced immunity is weaker than natural immunity to the measles disease. Around the Web Men Over 40 Are Ditching The Blue Pill for This 7x Stronger Alternative HartfordReviewJournal Protein Isn't Enough - Here's What Really Builds Muscle After 60 ApexLabs If You're Collecting Under $3950 In SS a Month: You're Eligible for These 10 ... SilverPenny “For the first time in a generation, American babies are being born infected with measles and the science told us this was coming,” Michelle Perro, a pediatrician told the Defender. “The vaccine program didn’t eliminate measles — it shifted the risk to the most vulnerable: newborns and the pregnant women carrying them.” These physicians claim that women who had a natural measles infection as a child are better protected against giving birth to a baby with congenital measles than women who got the MMR vaccine, the Defender reported. The MMR vaccine was first introduced to the United States’ markets in 1971 as a combined measles, mumps and rubella shot, however it did not get widely adopted until the 1980’s when the World Health Organization starting expanding vaccination initiatives, according to the WHO. Before the MMR vaccine, measles was a “routine childhood illness,” where over 99 percent of children made it through the disease with “no problem, resulting in robust, permanent and life-long immunity,” family practitioner Dr. Ben Edwards told the Defender. The Pennsylvania Department of Health was not able to provide comment to the DCNF before publication. “Practically all pregnant mothers had measles in their youth,” Edwards said to the outlet. “It’s really a beautifully designed natural immunization process — but we’ve lost that because of the measles vaccine program.” ADVERTISEMENT Around the Web Protein Isn't Enough - Here's What Really Builds Muscle After 60 ApexLabs Edwards runs a private practice serving roughly 2,000 patients and treated over 300 cases of measles during the West Texas measles outbreak, the defender reported. Edwards said that before the vaccine, mothers passed along protective antibodies to their babies in the womb, which protected the babies during the “more vulnerable first year of life.” Children would get measles sometime in the first decades of life and then develop their own robust, lifelong immunity, the doctor told the Defender. The American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) also acknowledges that congenital measles was exceedingly rare before the introduction of the MMR vaccine, the outlet reported. “Historical studies suggest that congenital measles was uncommon in the prevaccine era, when most pregnant women were previously infected with measles during childhood,” the AAP said in a FAQ about congenital measles. The AAP defines congenital measles as an infection acquired before birth in which a newborn tests positive for measles or develops symptoms within the first 10 days of life. There’s about a 25 percent to 30 percent chance a pregnant woman who contracts measles will transmit the virus to her unborn child, the AAP said. Yifare Pennsylvania continues to boast high numbers of measles cases, with 1,487 confirmed reports and six measles or “measles-associated” deaths as of Nov. 6, the Defender reported. Two of these deaths were reportedly of infants, but it is unknown whether they had congenital infections, according to the University of Minnesota’s Center for Infectious Disease Research and Policy (CIDRAP). Pennsylvania’s death count included a stillborn baby who tested positive for measles, suggesting a case of congenital measles, as Democratic Gov. Josh Shapiro announced during an August press conference. However, the coroner, Dr. Stephen Diamantoni, told the Philadelphia Inquirer the child died of a ruptured spleen — not from measles. The Children’s Health Defense is a non-profit that was chaired by now Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. from 2015 to 2023. 关闭侧边栏 视频 健康风格 家庭饮食文化 生活店 关闭侧边栏 菜单 ABC News 育儿 2026 年 11 月 10 日 宾夕法尼亚州孕妇中 麻疹 病例不断增加:如何了解风险 观看:随着美国即将失去消除状态,麻疹 病例排名前 5,000 例

规模、影响与风险

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles cases grow among pregnant women in Pennsylvania: What to know about risks - Good Morning America

News Brief
Measles cases among pregnant women in Pennsylvania have surged to 97 in 2026, up from 16 in 2025 and 6 in 2024. This poses significant risks to both mothers and their unborn babies, including pneumonia, premature birth, and low birth weight. While there is no specific treatment for congenital measles, newborns can receive immunoglobulin to lessen the severity or risk of death. The Pennsylvania Department of Health is working on standardized reporting and tracking tools for congenital measles cases.
Original source text (English)
Close the sidebar Video Wellness Style Family Food Culture Living Shop Close the sidebar Menu ABC News Parenting November 10, 2026 Measles cases grow among pregnant women in Pennsylvania: What to know about risks WATCH: Measles cases top 5,000 as US set to lose elimination status By Dr. Jade Cobern Measles cases in Pennsylvania have now surpassed 1,500, according to data reported on Monday. As the outbreak continues to grow, doctors and health officials are concerned about a rising number of cases among pregnant women, posing a risk to both mom and baby. As of Nov. 6, "the Pennsylvania Department of Health (DOH) has now confirmed that 97 of the state's measles cases in 2026 were in pregnant women," Stephanie Nojiri, the DOH deputy press secretary, told ABC News. There were only 16 total measles cases in the state in 2025 and 6 total in 2024, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). "Because measles was declared eliminated in the United States decades ago, modern data related to measles during pregnancy and the related risks is limited, but clinical studies and systematic reviews provide clear statistical evidence of the dangers associated with contracting measles while pregnant," the PA DOH said in a statement. "The 97 cases of measles confirmed in pregnant women thus far in 2026 is the highest number of cases in that demographic in Pennsylvania in decades," the state department of health said. "Pennsylvania has not seen an overall outbreak of this size in 35 years." The department added it "has provided clinical consultation for pregnant women with measles throughout the outbreak." The PA DOH released guidance about measles in pregnancy in a health alert last month. In addition to posing a risk to the mother's health, measles infection in pregnancy can be transmitted to the baby before birth, leading to congenital measles. Risks of measles to mom and baby The CDC warns that measles infection during pregnancy can lead to complications including pneumonia and death, as well as pregnancy loss, premature delivery, and low birth weight for their babies. "You have to watch the mother fetus dyad so closely because of the risk of fetal death," Dr. Sharee Livingston, an OBGYN at UPMC Magee-Womens in Central Pennsylvania, told ABC News. "You're paying attention to things like growth restriction and ensuring that there's fetal well-being, so that you can intervene with delivery if the fetus is viable." Livingston said she has taken care of several pregnant women with measles in this outbreak. "That's a scary place to be for the patient, for the supporter, for the healthcare provider to know that you have a person with a communicable disease that is preventable, and that could put both her and her fetus at risk," Livingston said. "It's a very challenging position to be in." If transmitted in utero, a baby can be born with congenital measles, "characterized by a febrile rash syndrome within 10 days of birth," the CDC says. These babies are at risk of many health complications including pneumonia, encephalitis, and death. Dr. Paul Offit, director of the Vaccine Education Center at the Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, said the risk of congenial measles is highest when the baby is delivered within 10 days of the mother developing a rash from measles. If the baby survives in the short term, a congenital measles infection places them at higher risk of later developing a progressive and fatal neurological condition called subacute sclerosing panencephalitis (SSPE), the CDC says. SSPE is characterized by "a deterioration in social skills, deterioration in personality, deterioration in handwriting. You descend into a vegetative state and die," Offit said. "The difference for congenital measles is you don't have to wait five or seven years. It can happen in a few months. It can happen within two years." Overall, cases of congenital measles have been very rare in the United States. Offit explained that congenital measles has historically been very rare because most adults were immune to the virus from infection in childhood before widespread immunization. "We're learning about congenital measles. We really are," Offit said. "In the measles era, meaning before the [1963] vaccine ... Children would get infected, so they wouldn't get infected as adults." Now, Offit said we are seeing the impacts of a growing adult population who are not immune to the virus. "You have people in their 20s, 30s, and 40s now who really didn't grow up in the age of measles," Offit said. "They are extremely unlikely they would have been naturally infected, and they've chosen not to vaccinate. So now, what you have that I didn't have when I was a child was a fairly large population of vulnerable adults." Tracking cases of congenital measles is also complicated by lack of standardized reporting in the U.S. The PA DOH said in a statement there is "no national public health case definition for standardized reporting or tracking of congenital measles cases." The department added, "DOH is working closely with our colleagues at the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) to develop standard definitions and tracking tools for congenital measles cases." Treatment and prevention The CDC says pregnant women should not receive the MMR vaccine because it contains some live virus, but they can receive immunoglobulin antibodies. This can reduce the risk of infection if they are non-immune and exposed to measles in pregnancy or to help lessen the severity of illness if infected. There is no specific treatment for congenital measles, but newborns can also receive immunoglobulin if they are born to a mother with measles which may help lessen the severity or risk of death from congenital measles, the CDC says. Dr. Holly W. Cummings, an OBGYN and chair of the Pennsylvania section of the American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists, said that preconception counseling about vaccines is very important to help prevent more pregnant women from getting measles. "Optimum pregnancy care for everybody, even outside of the context of a measles outbreak, is to have preconception care," Cummings said. "See your healthcare provider before you get pregnant, so that you can talk about any health planning that needs to be taken into account before pregnancy. One of those things we routinely think about before pregnancy is vaccination status." It is also recommended for the people around pregnant women and young babies to be up to date on vaccines too, adding an extra layer of protection for them. "If you're a family member with a pregnant woman in your family, she would no longer be able to be vaccinated, but other people in the family could be," Cummings said. "So that's the next best thing for creating a safe environment for the pregnant woman and the unborn fetus and the eventual baby that's going to join the family." Jade A. Cobern, MD, MPH, is a practicing physician, board-certified in pediatrics and general preventive medicine, and is a fellow of the ABC News Medical Unit. Dr. Elizabeth Herrin also contributed to this story.
