B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles spreads again in Ohio as US may soon lose elimination status

News Brief
Ohio is experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with cases increasing by 21% in the past week. The state has reported 327 cases in 26 counties, and the national total of confirmed measles cases has reached 5,182 in 45 states, the highest annual total since 1991. Health officials warn that the United States may lose its measles elimination status, emphasizing the importance of the MMR vaccine to prevent the disease, which is preventable and can be deadly.
Original source text (English)
Nationwide ground stop caused by aging, 'overloaded' air traffic control systems, officials say Find us on Google 📌 America's birthday 🎂 Start the day smarter ☀️ Get the USA TODAY app U.S. Politics Sports Entertainment Life Money Travel Opinion Crossword ONLY AT USA TODAY: Newsletters For Subscribers Real Estate From the Archives USA TODAY PLAY Crossword Marvel Comics eNewspaper Magazines Investigations Video Podcasts Humankind Advertise Your Business WITNESS (True Crime) Just Curious Best-selling Booklist Top Workplaces Nomination Legals Press Releases Cars Boats Grocery Shopping Holidays OUR PORTFOLIO: Real Estate 10BEST USA TODAY Store Shopping Money Essentials WITNESS (True Crime) Southern Kitchen Home Internet Services HEALTH Ohio Department of Health Add Topic Measles spreads again in Ohio as US may soon lose elimination status Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Nov. 13, 2026, 11:10 a.m. ET Hear this story Ohio is experiencing its third measles outbreak of 2026, with cases increasing by 21% in the past week. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, putting the country's measles elimination status at risk. Health officials state the MMR vaccine is the best way to prevent measles, which can be a deadly disease. As one person may have spread measles across seven locations in two Ohio counties, yet another county joined the list of confirmed cases as the state's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues. And the United States is in danger of losing its measles elimination status as the World Health Organization was set to meet. Ohio added 17 new cases in the past week, a 21% increase from the 14 new cases reported the week before, the Ohio Department of Health reports. ODH is reporting 327 cases in 26 counties so far this year, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Nov. 12. That number is more than six times (626.7%) more than the 45 cases reported in all of 2025. Mahoning County joined the list this week with one case, and Ashtabula reported the most new cases with six. Measles cases nationwide have hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. As of Nov. 5, the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention reported 5,182 confirmed measles cases in 45 states plus Washington, D.C. – more than double last year's total of 2,289, USA TODAY reports. That's the highest annual total since 1991. The vast majority of cases in Ohio are among unvaccinated individuals, per ODH. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Measles growing in Ohio. These counties report cases in 2026 Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashland: 8 Ashtabula: 53 (six new cases) Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 8 Gallia: 3 Geauga: 56 (two new cases) Hancock: 1 Harrison: 18 Holmes: 10 Knox: 5 Mahoning: 1 (one new case; first time on the list) Medina: 28 (one new case) Meigs: 4 Mercer: 4 Morgan: 3 Morrow: 13 Ottawa: 1 Pike: 24 (one new case) Portage: 1 Stark: 1 Trumbull: 15 (two new cases) Tuscarawas: 15 (two new cases) Van Wert: 3 Vinton: 1 Wayne: 46 (one new case) Wood: 1 Unknown: 1 (one new case) The case count for the year reflects previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with one case in 2023, seven in 2024 and 45 cases in 2025. Weekly Ohio measles cases by the numbers Total cases in Ohio this year have continued climbing, as reported by ODH: 310 cases in 25 counties on Nov. 5 296 cases in 24 counties on Oct. 29 260 cases in 24 counties on Oct. 22 252 cases in 23 counties on Oct. 15 228 cases in 21 counties on Oct. 8 206 cases in 20 counties on Oct. 1 196 cases in 16 counties on Sept. 24 180 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 17 133 cases in 15 counties on Sept. 10 122 cases in 14 counties on Sept. 3 107 cases in 12 counties on Aug. 27 99 cases in 12 counties Aug. 20 69 cases in 11 counties on Aug. 13 45 cases in 10 counties on Aug. 6 Measles kills six in Pennsylvania; infant dies of measles in Wisconsin Pennsylvania confirmed its sixth measles-related death of the year on Nov. 4, while New York announced its first measles death in 30 years on Nov. 2, according to USA TODAY. Wisconsin also reported a grim milestone on Nov. 5, with health officials saying that its first measles death since 1990 was that of an infant. Measles cases on the rise as Ohio vaccination rates fluctuate in state, U.S. Vaccination rates for measles have declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Ohio's vaccination rate for the 2025-26 school year slipped well below the herd immunity threshold, making it susceptible to outbreaks. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventive measures, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine. Trump signs executive order to separate MMR vaccine President Donald Trump in August 2026 signed an executive order to split the childhood measles, mumps and rubella vaccine into three shots and delivering them at separate medical visits as well as "calling for more vaccine flexibility and research" and fewer childhood vaccinations, according to USA TODAY. "You have the MMR, we want it in three separate vaccinations, given at separate times. Together there could be a possibility they're quite lethal and separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal, but just very effective," said Trump from the Oval Office as he signed the order. But the MMR vaccine is not lethal, and medical research shows no health benefit or reduced risk from splitting it. In contrast, separating the shots increases injection pain, leaves children unprotected longer, and lowers overall vaccination rates, according to experts. The MMR vaccine has been in existence since 1971, according to the World Health Organization. Measles cases hit 35-year high as US may lose 'eliminated' status The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Falling vaccination rates and the resulting resurgence, however, have raised concerns that the United States could lose its measles elimination status, a designation awarded when a disease has not spread continuously within a country for at least 12 months. The WHO was expected to review the U.S. status on Nov. 10 following the 2025 Texas outbreak that killed three people. Canada already lost its "measles-free" status in November 2025. Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but it can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before through four days after a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. Featured Weekly Ad

South Carolina ranks second in measles cases as outbreak continues WJCL US LIVE Current Time 0:00 / Total Duration 1:10 Sorry, this video is not available, please check back later. Advertisement Updated: 8:26 AM EST Nov 12, 2026 Editorial Standards i Madelyn Cooper Reporter/Producer South Carolina ranks second in measles cases, doctor warns of contagious virus Updated: 8:26 AM EST Nov 12, 2026 Editorial Standards i Madelyn Cooper Reporter/Producer BEAUFORT, S.C. — Measles cases continue to be reported in South Carolina following a major outbreak earlier this year, with the state ranking second nationwide for confirmed cases, according to the Center for Disease Control and Prevention. "It's a virus. It is probably one of the most, if not the most contagious disease," said Dr. Kurt Gambla, chief medical officer at Beaufort Memorial. Advertisement Gambla explained that measles can initially resemble the common cold, but a rash typically develops a few days later. "In some rare cases, it can be a fatal disease," Gambla said. The South Carolina Department of Public Health reported 997 cases during the 2026 outbreak. As of the CDC's November 5 update, the state ranks second nationwide for measles cases, with 670 confirmed cases. Gambla noted that more than 4,800 cases have been reported nationwide through October, which is double the number from last year. "We thought that, the outbreak in Spartanburg, we had that kind of resolved by the spring. But there have been some sporadic cases since then," Gambla said. Health experts advise individuals who suspect they may have measles to call ahead before visiting the emergency department to allow staff to prepare. "There are certain precautions for isolation. The only way to confirm it is through a test. Either a swab or blood test or urine test," Gambla said. Vaccination remains the most effective way to protect against measles, Gambla emphasized. "One dose will give 93% immunity. Two doses, 97% immunity," Gambla said. Gambla said treatment focuses on staying hydrated and managing symptoms, as there is no cure for measles and the virus must run its course. “巨大的倒退”:在 RFK Jr 的领导下,美国可能会失去 麻疹 的淘汰地位 - 《卫报》

‘A tremendous step backwards’: US could lose measles elimination status under RFK Jr’s leadership - The Guardian
新闻简报美国正面临自 1991 年以来最严重的 麻疹 麻疹,已有 5,400 多例病例和 6 人死亡。卫生部长小罗伯特·F·肯尼迪 (Robert F. Kennedy Jr.) 因其 疫苗 政策而受到批评,该政策导致 疫苗接种 费率大幅下降。麻疹 麻疹 具有高度传染性,三分之二的感染者是成年人,并且有先天性 麻疹 的潜力。CDC 的应对能力受到严重阻碍,消除状态的丧失可能会对普通美国人产生实际影响,需要 疫苗接种 或隔离证明。

地点与对象
照片:Jan Sonnenmair/Getty Images 全屏查看图片 麻疹 被认为是医学上已知的最具传染性的疾病之一。
事件
由于自 1991 年以来最严重的 麻疹 麻疹(已报告病例超过 5,400 例),美国可能会失去 麻疹 消除状态。卫生部长罗伯特·F. (Robert F.) 因其 疫苗 政策而受到批评。
规模、影响与风险
麻疹 已导致六人死亡,麻疹 被认为是最具传染性的疾病之一。风险很高,三分之二的感染者是成年人,并且有可能患有先天性 麻疹。疾病预防控制中心的应对能力受到严重阻碍。
应对、状态与不确定性
一个独立小组将考虑流行病学证据,并由泛美卫生组织主任做出最终决定。失去消除资格将影响普通美国人,需要 疫苗接种 或隔离证明。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

‘A tremendous step backwards’: US could lose measles elimination status under RFK Jr’s leadership - The Guardian

News Brief
The United States is facing its worst measles outbreak since 1991, with over 5,400 cases and six deaths. Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. is criticized for his vaccine policies, which have led to a significant decrease in vaccination rates. The measles outbreak is highly infectious, with two-thirds of infected individuals being adults, and the potential for congenital measles. The CDC's capacity to respond has been significantly hampered, and the loss of elimination status could have real effects on everyday Americans, requiring proof of vaccination or quarantine.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: Jan Sonnenmair/Getty Images View image in fullscreen Measles is considered one of the most infectious diseases known to medicine. Photograph: Jan Sonnenmair/Getty Images ‘A tremendous step backwards’: US could lose measles elimination status under RFK Jr’s leadership Country is in the middle of worst measles outbreak since 1991, with more than 5,400 cases nationally The United States could lose its measles elimination status this week, an event likely to cement health secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr’s legacy of meddling in vaccine policies amid the worst outbreak of the virus in the US in 35 years. If the US loses its measles-free status, it would join Canada and several European and Asian nations that had eliminated the disease only for it to return. “That is a tremendous step backwards,” said Dr Paul Offit, director of the Vaccine Education Center, an attending physician in the division of infectious diseases at Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia, and a prominent critic of Kennedy. A panel of eight independent experts will consider the epidemiological evidence in Washington DC, part of a group called the Pan American Health Organization (PAHO), a regional branch of the World Health Organization (WHO). The PAHO director will make the final call. The vaccine limbo in the US is worsening the measles outbreak Read more “Independent of what PAHO says, measles is now endemic in the US. Measles has a foothold in the US,” said Offit. “We’re going to see it this year, we’re going to see it next year,” because the US “critically decreased the percentage” of people vaccinated. The US is in the midst of its worst measles outbreak since 1991 with more than 5,400 cases nationally, according to the Johns Hopkins University International Vaccine Access Center tracker. Just this year, six people have died in Pennsylvania, a child died in New York on election day and an infant died in Wisconsin days later. Nearly every US state has an active outbreak. Measles is considered one of the most infectious diseases known to medicine. The overwhelming majority of Americans vaccinate themselves and their children, but the sheer contagiousness of measles means even small dips in immunity allow it to spread. It kills one to three people per 1,000 people and hospitalizes about one-in-five unvaccinated people who are infected. Two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine is 97% effective against the disease. Although elimination status is a formal public health designation, its loss has real effects on everyday Americans. In just one example, Dr David Margolius, the Cleveland, Ohio, director of public health, is warning people to find proof of their measles vaccination in the event they’re exposed to a measles-positive patient. “Now is the time to go and find that evidence, because now this is a real day-to-day life thing that could happen to you,” said Margolius, who is also a director-at-large of the Big Cities Health Coalition. Without proof, patients face either an immunology blood draw or a 21-day quarantine. Cleveland health authorities have already done a tremendous amount of detective work this year to track down people exposed to measles patients in hospital waiting rooms and elevators. “We reach out to people and for most people, including myself, we have no personal experience with measles,” he said. “They’ve got everything else going on in their life, and now they’re getting this call.” The types of people who contract measles has also changed. In 1963, when the vaccine was first licensed, measles was considered a childhood disease. Most children contracted the disease by the age of five. “Now you’re seeing the epidemiology shift,” said Offit. Two-thirds of people infected with measles in Pennsylvania are adults, a group on which researchers have little information. Mothers have even given birth to babies with measles. “Now, you’re seeing something we just didn’t see at all – congenital measles.” The potential loss of elimination status comes at a tenuous moment for the Trump administration. Republicans just endured a historic drubbing in the midterm elections. Several new Democratic doctors and scientists are slated to come into Congress on promises to hold Kennedy accountable. At the same time, Trump has doubled down on his commitment to vaccine antagonism with the appointment of Stephen Miller as chief of staff. “Next to me, Stephen Miller is the strongest Maha supporter in the administration,” Kennedy said in a statement about Miller’s appointment. “It’s time to unleash the Kraken.” Meanwhile, the capacity of the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) to respond has been significantly hampered by the Trump administration. The agency has lost up to one-third of its staff, Kennedy has interfered in the agency’s reporting of measles deaths and has spread misinformation about the measles vaccines. Even so, Margolius doesn’t lay all the blame at Kennedy’s feet. Vaccine hesitancy was increasing before Trump took office, and pandemic-era disruptions were real. “Some of the narrative has been to pin this on our health secretary, which certainly I think he could do much more to help the situation rather than hurting the situation, but I do think the case count is climbing everywhere,” he said. “This could happen even under a more pro-public health administration.” The second Trump administration also made broad changes that challenge measles elimination globally. Some of Trump’s first acts in 2024 included withdrawal from the World Health Organization (WHO) and the Global Vaccine Alliance (GAVI). GAVI provides measles vaccines to nations such as India and Malawi. There are about 95,000 measles deaths globally, according to the World Health Organization, mostly in unvaccinated children under five years old. At home, Republicans also enacted the largest ever federal cut to health insurance, changes expected to result in more than 17 million people losing insurance through 2034. That has challenged people such as Margolius, who struggle to connect people with vaccines when their health insurance coverage is unstable. “Until we can rejoin the WHO and cooperate with other health agencies all over the world ... This will continue,” said Margolius. This article was amended on 11 November 2026. Polk County confirms case of measles in a child - The Des Moines Register

查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 11 月 11 日至 2026 年 11 月 11 日。
地点与对象
美国爱荷华州波尔克县。

波尔克县卫生部门确认了一名儿童 麻疹 病例。截至2026年11月11日,该州已报告2026年麻疹病例50例。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Polk County confirms case of measles in a child - The Des Moines Register

News Brief
The Polk County Health Department confirmed a case of measles in a child, bringing the total number of measles cases in Iowa to 50 in 2026. Public health officials are urging people to monitor for symptoms and ensure vaccination, as the virus is highly contagious and can lead to serious complications, especially in children.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Opinion Business Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals IOWA Polk County confirms case of measles in a child Kyle Werner Des Moines Register Nov. 11, 2026Updated Nov. 12, 2026, 8:16 a.m. CT The Polk County Health Department confirmed a case of measles in a child on Wednesday, Nov. 11. Officials have confirmed 50 cases of measles in Iowa in 2026 as of Nov. 5. Iowa Health and Human Services announced the first measles case of 2026 in July, and confirmed 17 cases at the end of August. In 2025, the state reported just nine cases. Public health officials have evidence that this measles outbreak was spread from person to person. They're encouraging people to identify illnesses, share information and ensure access to vaccination. Places and times infected individuals could have spread measles in Iowa Potential measles exposure locations and times include: Walmart: 1002 National Dr., Ankeny; Thursday, Nov. 5, between 5 and 8 p.m. Hy-Vee: 410 N. Ankeny Blvd., Ankeny; Friday, Nov. 6, between noon and 3 p.m. UnityPoint Clinic Urgent Care: 3625 N. Ankeny Blvd., Ankeny; Sunday, Nov. 8 between 10:10 a.m. and 1:15 p.m. UnityPoint Health – Iowa Methodist Medical Center/Blank Children's Hospital Emergency Department: 1200 Pleasant St., Des Moines (times listed at specific locations below): Iowa Methodist Medical Center/Blank Children's Hospital Emergency Department waiting room between 11:25 a.m. and 1:35 p.m. Blank Children’s Hospital Emergency Department between 11:30 a.m. and 5:20 p.m. Hallway from Blank Children’s Emergency Department to Blank Children’s inpatient elevators between 3:15 and 5:25 p.m. Blank Children’s Hospital 4th floor between 3:20 and 10:45 p.m. The Polk County Health Department asks those who visited the locations to take an online survey to track potential exposures. What is measles? What are the symptoms for measles? Measles is a viral illness with symptoms that include fever, cough, red and watery eyes, a runny nose and a rash. Symptoms may appear from 7 to 14 days after contact with someone who is infected and contagious. The virus is spread through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes and breathes and can remain virulent for up to two hours. According to the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, 90% of people who come into contact with the virus become infected if they are unvaccinated, making it one of the most contagious diseases known. Additionally, 1 in 5 of those people end up hospitalized, according to the CDC. Measles can cause health complications including ear infections and diarrhea, as well as pneumonia, swelling of the brain and in some cases, death. Children are most at risk for serious complications. In 2025, there was a total of nine measles cases in Iowa and 2,289 across the country, with 243 patients hospitalized and three deaths, according to Iowa HHS. How can I avoid getting measles? Measles is a vaccine-preventable disease. The MMR vaccine (measles, mumps and rubella) protects against measles with 97% efficacy, and is typically administered to children as part of the regular vaccine course, with the first dose when the child is 12 to 15 months of age and the second at 4 to 6 years. "With measles continuing to circulate in the region, Iowa HHS anticipates that additional cases may occur, particularly among individuals who are not protected against measles through vaccination or prior infection," HHS said in a news release. The recent increase in measles outbreaks has been attributed to plummeting vaccination rates. What to do if you believe you are infected with measles Those who may have been exposed to the infected individual are asked to monitor for symptoms for up to 21 days and to call their medical provider or the nearest emergency room before arrival and tell them they have been exposed to measles and have symptoms. Register reporters Norah Judson and Lucia Cheng contributed to this report. Kyle Werner is the breaking news and public safety reporter for the Register. Reach him at kwerner@registermedia.com. News Brief

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

塞尔维亚 麻疹:27 例,其中 18 例在博尔 - srpske.rs

Measles in Serbia: 27 Cases, 18 of Them in Bor - srpske.rs
新闻简报塞尔维亚报告了 27 例 麻疹 病例,其中 18 例发生在博尔市。塞尔维亚公共卫生研究所“Milan Jovanovic Batut 博士”证实了这些病例,自 10 月初以来已发现 22 例病例。该疾病已蔓延到波扎雷瓦茨和贝尔格莱德等其他地方。疫苗接种 至关重要,95% 以上的覆盖率被认为对群体免疫至关重要。患者越年轻,出现肺炎、脑炎等并发症的风险就越高。
查看新闻五要素
时间
扎耶卡尔的流行病学家如是说。
地点与对象
塞尔维亚,博尔市,波扎雷瓦茨,贝尔格莱德。
事件
塞尔维亚报告了 27 例 麻疹 病例,博尔报告了 18 例;其他三个地点出现疑似病例。
规模、影响与风险
10月初以来有22例,其中博尔市有18例;1000 名患者中出现 6-8 例中耳感染、1-6 例肺炎、1-2 例脑炎以及罕见的严重晚期并发症。
应对、状态与不确定性
调查和 疫苗接种 活动正在进行中;预计当天即可得到实验室结果。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles in Serbia: 27 Cases, 18 of Them in Bor - srpske.rs

News Brief
Serbia has reported 27 measles cases, with 18 in the City of Bor. The Institute of Public Health of Serbia “Dr Milan Jovanovic Batut” confirmed the cases, noting 22 cases since early October. The disease has spread to other locations including Pozarevac and Belgrade. Vaccination is crucial, with coverage above 95% considered essential for herd immunity. The younger the patient, the higher the risk of complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis.
Original source text (English)
November 14, 2026 · Beograd Newsroom English €117,62din-0.01%Exchange ratesMarkets ›7°BelgradeForecast › Search⏎ Search Go Politics Sport Economy World Crime Culture Showbiz Lifestyle Technology Science Opinion Weather Exchange rates EnglishNewsroom Theme Politics Sport Economy World Crime Culture Showbiz Lifestyle Technology Science Opinion TRENDING| Veljko Raznatovic to pull children from Titel kindergarten→ Home / Health Health Measles in Serbia: 27 Cases, 18 of Them in Bor Batut reports data through November 8: 22 cases since early October, including a health worker from Pozarevac. What an epidemiologist from Zajecar says. Jelena Đurić·November 10, 2026·2 min read·0 PodeliKopiraj link Illustrative photo for a report on measles in Serbia/ Photo: Pixabay Should you worry about measles? If you have a child who is not vaccinated, there is reason to pay attention. The Institute of Public Health of Serbia “Dr Milan Jovanovic Batut” announced on November 10 that 27 measles cases have been registered in Serbia since the start of the year, through November 8. Most are in Bor. What exactly does Batut say? 22 cases have been registered since the beginning of October. The institute states in its release: “Since the beginning of October, 22 cases of measles have been registered.” Of those, 18 are part of the outbreak reported for the City of Bor on November 4. The other four October cases: two in Belgrade one in Pozarevac one in the area covered by the Public Health Institute in Kosovska Mitrovica The Pozarevac case is a health worker who had contact with a patient from Bor at the Pozarevac General Hospital. Earlier in the year, one case each was recorded in Novi Pazar (April) and Belgrade (May), along with three in the Kosovska Mitrovica institute’s area during August and September. How did the disease spread in Bor? Epidemiologist Svetlana Zivkovic of the “Timok” Public Health Institute in Zajecar described the sequence to RTS. The first to fall ill was an unvaccinated child under one year old, too young for the vaccine. Another child of that age followed, then older unvaccinated children, and finally parents who were either unvaccinated or had not received all doses. Bor currently has 18 confirmed cases, and three more are suspected. Zivkovic said she expected the laboratory results the same day. Why does vaccination matter? Zivkovic says vaccination is the most important measure for stopping the spread of the disease, and that it also protects people who cannot receive it. Coverage is the deciding factor: “It is considered that vaccine coverage should be above 95 percent, and the closer it is to 100 percent, the better the herd immunity,” Zivkovic told RTS. The “Timok” institute and the Children’s Clinic of the Bor Health Center went through vaccination records and compiled lists of eligible children. Invitations are already going out. Zivkovic urges every child who has reached the prescribed age and has no temporary or permanent contraindications to respond. Which complications does Batut list? Young children are the most vulnerable, and pneumonia is the most common complication. Batut says complications are more frequent in children under five, adults over 20 and people with weakened immunity. Figures from the release: middle ear infection: 6 to 8 in 100 patients pneumonia: 1 to 6 in 100 patients encephalitis: 1 to 2 in 1,000 patients There is also a rare, severe late complication, subacute sclerosing panencephalitis, which appears years after infection. The younger the patient at the time of illness, the higher the risk. What can parents of infants do? The virus spreads easily through the air, so the epidemiologist has specific advice for those whose children are too young to be vaccinated: “I advise parents to avoid crowds, gatherings and long stays in enclosed spaces with very small children, those under one year old,” Zivkovic said. 供应缺口导致的可预防系统故障引发孟加拉国 麻疹 复苏 - Bioengineer.org

Preventable Systems Failures Driven by Supply Gaps Trigger Bangladesh Measles Resurgence - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报2026 年,孟加拉国经历了严重的 麻疹 暴发,疑似病例超过 50,500 例,至少 415 人死亡,主要影响五岁以下儿童,尤其是城市贫民窟和难民营的儿童。此次 暴发 是由系统性行政中断和 疫苗 供应短缺驱动的,导致群体免疫力受到严重侵蚀。这种情况突出表明迫切需要强有力的免疫计划和持续的 疫苗 供应,以防止未来出现 暴发。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该分析由医学博士撰写。
地点与对象
孟加拉国,特别是首都达卡和考克斯巴扎尔的罗兴亚难民营。
事件
严重的 麻疹 暴发 疑似病例超过 50,500 例,至少 415 例确诊或疑似死亡,其特征是可预防的系统故障。
规模、影响与风险
50,500 suspected cases, 415 deaths, 20 million children partially or entirely unprotected, severe localized burdens in urban slums and humanitarian zones, high risk of secondary complications.
应对、状态与不确定性
经调查,由于行政和后勤方面持续存在挑战,自 2020 年以来没有在全国范围内开展补充免疫活动。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Preventable Systems Failures Driven by Supply Gaps Trigger Bangladesh Measles Resurgence - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
Bangladesh experienced a severe measles outbreak in 2026, with over 50,500 suspected cases and at least 415 deaths, primarily affecting children under five, especially in urban slums and refugee camps. This outbreak was driven by systemic administrative disruptions and vaccine supply shortages, leading to a significant erosion of herd immunity. The situation highlights the critical need for robust immunization programs and continuous vaccine supply to prevent future outbreaks.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Saturday, November 14, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Preventable Systems Failures Driven by Supply Gaps Trigger Bangladesh Measles Resurgence by November 12, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 6 mins read 0 Preventable Systems Failures Driven by Supply Gaps Trigger Bangladesh Measles Resurgence Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A perspective analysis published in Discover Public Health reports that Bangladesh has confronted its most severe measles outbreak in decades during 2026, documenting more than 50,500 suspected cases and at least 415 confirmed or suspected measles-related deaths between mid-March and mid-May. The analysis, authored by Md. Abdus Salam, characterizes this widespread public health crisis not as an unavoidable biological occurrence, but as a preventable systems failure that reversed years of hard-won progress toward disease elimination. Historically recognized as an international benchmark for immunization programming in resource-constrained environments, the country experienced an erosion of protective herd immunity when systemic administrative disruptions, nationwide vaccine supply shortages, and prolonged intervals between supplemental vaccination campaigns converged to expose expanding cohorts of unprotected children. The historical trajectory of measles control in Bangladesh highlights the scale of the recent setback. In the early 2000s, measles persisted as a major cause of pediatric mortality across the country, with national coverage for the first dose of measles-containing vaccine stagnating at or below 75 percent and zero administrative districts reaching the 95 percent coverage threshold necessary to interrupt transmission. Sustained national investments in the Expanded Programme on Immunization, combined with integrated child health days, routine vitamin A distribution, and recurring nationwide supplementary immunization activities, gradually lifted first-dose administrative coverage from approximately 74 to 89 percent in 2000 to between 94 and 118 percent by 2016. In parallel, the introduction of a routine second measles-containing vaccine dose in 2012 expanded second-dose coverage from roughly one-third of eligible children to more than 90 percent by the mid-2010s, driving laboratory-confirmed measles incidence down by more than 80 percent from approximately 40 cases per million population to 6 per million. Discover more Health health Get Health Tips Despite these extensive historical gains, national surveillance data compiled by domestic health agencies and international monitoring bodies revealed an underlying accumulation of susceptible individuals starting after 2019. National coverage estimates for both the first and second doses of the combined measles-rubella vaccine plateaued and subsequently exhibited modest declines. First-dose coverage slipped from approximately 88 to 89 percent down to 86 percent, while second-dose coverage dropped from roughly 89 percent to 80.7 percent by the 2023 to 2024 monitoring period, leaving an estimated 20 million children partially or entirely unprotected against infection. Compounding these gradual routine reductions, Bangladesh failed to conduct any nationwide measles-rubella supplementary immunization activities following its 2020 campaign, departing from its historical schedule of four-yearly catch-up exercises due to operational disruptions caused by the COVID-19 pandemic and subsequent domestic political instability. Discover more Check Symptoms public health Public Health Track Outbreaks The vulnerability deepened considerably between 2024 and 2025 following a nationwide stockout of the measles-rubella vaccine, which the perspective links to procedural shifts in procurement arrangements and administrative delays that accompanied a broader political transition. A planned nationwide supplementary campaign originally scheduled for 2024 was postponed, remained unimplemented throughout 2025, and was ultimately cancelled altogether. While international evaluations by the World Health Organization and UNICEF recorded routine coverage figures of 97 percent for the first dose and 93 percent for the second dose in 2023, certain internal national reports cited an acute drop to approximately 56 to 59 percent coverage by 2025. Although the author notes that discrepancies between administrative datasets and independent field surveys preclude an absolute causal determination, the accumulated evidence indicates that these procurement disruptions expanded the pool of susceptible hosts precisely when routine delivery systems were operating under strain. When transmission expanded rapidly in early 2026, the infectious pathogen exploited these accumulated immunity gaps with severe clinical consequences. The National International Health Regulations Focal Point formally notified the World Health Organization of an accelerating nationwide increase in measles cases on April 4, 2026. Directorate General of Health Services data recorded 50,500 suspected cases and 415 deaths by May 11, reflecting a crude reported death-to-suspected-case ratio of approximately 0.82 percent. Epidemiological tracking between April 22 and May 4 documented cumulative suspected cases surging from 27,164 to 41,793, laboratory-confirmed infections rising from 3,934 to 5,467, and reported deaths increasing from 190 to 311. Approximately 79 to 80 percent of all reported infections occurred among children under five years of age, with 66 percent concentrated in children under two years and roughly one-third occurring in infants younger than nine months who were not yet eligible for standard routine measles immunization. The spatial distribution of cases revealed severe localized burdens, with the outbreak reaching 58 of Bangladesh’s 64 administrative districts across all eight divisions while concentrating heavily within specific urban slums, peripheral settlements, and humanitarian zones. In the capital division of Dhaka alone, situation reports documented more than 18,000 to 19,000 cases, heavily clustering within densely populated informal settlements including Demra, Jatrabari, Kamrangirchar, Korail, Mirpur, and Tejgaon. Within these crowded urban environments, high population density, economic precarity, and geographic mobility presented substantial barriers to routine healthcare delivery. Outside municipal centers, field monitoring in Gopalganj identified zero-dose children among highly mobile floating families who had been bypassed by both standard clinic-based schedules and targeted door-to-door immunization initiatives. Humanitarian settings presented another critical vulnerability during the 2026 resurgence, particularly within the congested Rohingya refugee camps established across Cox’s Bazar. Transmission within the camp complex escalated from 19 suspected cases in January to 199 in February, reaching 76 suspected cases with 8 confirmed infections by March. By mid-May, diagnostic testing confirmed 60 cases alongside 239 suspected cases and 5 attributable deaths, expanding to a cumulative total of 595 suspected cases and 60 laboratory-confirmed cases by May 20. The extreme transmissibility of the measles virus within these humanitarian enclaves was exacerbated by constrained baseline access to clinical services, widespread dependence on external humanitarian assistance, high rates of background malnutrition, and persistent cohorts of completely unvaccinated zero-dose infants residing in close quarters. The perspective emphasizes that the clinical severity of the outbreak was compounded by frequent secondary complications, most notably acute pneumonia, persistent diarrhea, and severe metabolic wasting linked directly to pre-existing childhood undernutrition and vitamin A deficiencies. Surging patient volumes placed severe operational strain on secondary and tertiary clinical centers, with daily hospital admissions frequently surpassing 1,200 to 1,300 patients. This influx rapidly overwhelmed pediatric wards and exposed structural deficits in facility-based isolation units, available health personnel, and laboratory diagnostic reagents. Clinical observations indicated that severe disease and fatal outcomes remained heavily concentrated among under-vaccinated or unvaccinated young children, demonstrating how nutritional deficits and immunization lapses synergistically worsen pediatric outcomes during large-scale community transmission. Bangladesh’s domestic outbreak mirrors a broader post-pandemic deterioration of measles control observed internationally. According to World Health Organization global monitoring figures, 59 countries experienced large or disruptive measles outbreaks in 2024—nearly three times the total recorded in 2021—including several jurisdictions that had previously attained verified elimination status. Worldwide, estimated coverage for the first vaccine dose reached approximately 84 to 86 percent in 2024, with second-dose uptake estimated at 76 percent, leaving global coverage well beneath the 95 percent threshold necessary to maintain community herd protection. Regionally, concurrent case increases and localized outbreaks were documented across India, Nepal, Thailand, and the Maldives, prompting public health authorities in neighboring Myanmar to reinforce cross-border surveillance and clinical preparedness along its frontier to mitigate potential cross-border transmission. In response to the crisis, the Government of Bangladesh joined with international partners, including the World Health Organization, UNICEF, and Gavi, to mount an emergency response campaign starting on April 5, 2026. Initially deployed across 30 high-risk upazilas in 18 districts, the intervention expanded into four city corporations before rolling out nationwide on April 20, strategically lowering the eligibility age to six months to shield vulnerable infants. Responders distributed more than 20 million vaccine doses, trained over 26,000 frontline health workers, and integrated vitamin A supplementation into clinical case management. Although administrative tracking recorded more than 18.3 million children vaccinated by May 19—surpassing initial national targets—an independent assessment published by the International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies on May 5 reported national administrative coverage at only 60.57 percent, noting that multiple districts had reached fewer than half of their target populations while active transmission continued. Concluding his evaluation, Salam argues that the 2026 epidemic serves as a clear indicator of structural fragility in primary health systems, illustrating that high national coverage metrics often obscure dangerous localized pockets of susceptibility. Rebuilding long-term resilience will require immediate stabilization of the national vaccine pipeline through secured buffer inventories, independent supplier verification mechanisms, and statutory protections that isolate essential medicine procurement from administrative or political transitions. Over the longer horizon, the perspective calls for institutionalizing regular supplementary catch-up campaigns, modernizing disease surveillance with real-time laboratory reporting, and prioritizing micro-planning in urban informal settlements and humanitarian camps to ensure sustained protection for marginalized and zero-dose populations before explosive outbreaks can emerge. Vermont health officials say they have confirmed connected cases of measles in Orleans County. They say the risk to the general public remains low, but that an investigation is ongoing. Vermont Health Commissioner Rick Hildebrant said the number of cases remained “less than five” for now, but characterized the situation as “evolving.” “We have the risk of seeing person-to-person transmission within the state of Vermont. We have not seen that yet. But I am fearful that we might,” he said in an interview Tuesday. Hildebrant said that the disease was believed to have been contracted when people traveled out-of-state but within the country. Measles cases have been occurring in Vermont more frequently in recent years as vaccination rates wane and the disease sees a resurgence nationwide. Hildebrant noted that this Vermont news had arrived in the same week as the Pan American Health Organization, an international public health agency, is expected to evaluate whether the U.S. should retain its measles elimination status. “That should never have happened. It shouldn't be happening. We are seeing deaths in America that we should not see,” the health commissioner said. Health officials in Vermont last detected a case of measles this February. The state also reported two cases in both 2025 and 2024. In the decade prior, Vermont had confirmed just one case in 2011 and one in 2018. The disease is highly contagious and spreads through the air. It can lead to dangerous and even fatal complications, like brain swelling and pneumonia, particularly in young children and those who aren’t vaccinated. About 1 in 5 unvaccinated people who get measles are hospitalized, according to the CDC. There is no cure for measles, but research shows the measles, mumps, and rubella — or MMR — vaccine is extremely effective at preventing the disease. “A single shot of MMR protects 93% against measles, and two is 97%. So while yes, general infection control practices are warranted, the reality is, given how contagious this illness is, that's not going to do it,” Hildebrant said. “You need to get vaccinated.” Measles shots are free in Vermont, even if someone is uninsured. The health department has published a list of resources to help Vermonters figure out if they’ve been immunized, and how to get vaccinated if they want to. Updated: November 10, 2026 at 1:21 PM EST This story has been updated with an interview with Vermont Health Commissioner Rick Hildebrant. Have questions, comments or tips? Send us a message. 00:00 00:00 08:27 2x1.5x1x Key Points The return of measles in the U.S. can carry a huge financial burden on the U.S. healthcare system. A new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates that the 2025 measles outbreak in the U.S. — which involved roughly 2,300 confirmed cases nationwide — carried an economic cost of more than $37 million. Those costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources spent investigating and containing outbreaks and wages lost when people have to miss work. Measles outbreaks aren’t just a public health threat. They can also be a financial burden on the U.S. — and the cost could only deepen as the once-eliminated disease spreads more widely. The U.S. is expected to lose its official measles elimination status following a review by the Pan American Health Organization this week, after cases ratcheted to recent highs this year. More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported nationwide in 2026 as of early November, marking the country’s worst outbreak in more than three decades. Eight deaths associated with the disease have been confirmed by states so far in 2026: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin. Based on this year’s case count, a new tool developed by researchers at Johns Hopkins University estimates an economic cost of nearly $83 million for the outbreaks. That total includes $56 million in public health response costs, almost $25 million in lost productivity and more than $1.8 million in direct medical expenses. The tool also estimates that 2025 measles outbreaks in the U.S. — which involved 2,289 confirmed cases nationwide – carried an economic cost of more than $37 million. The costs extend beyond treating people who become sick, accounting for the resources health departments spend investigating and containing outbreaks, as well as wages lost when people have to miss work. The tool uses more recent, state-specific cost and financing data than an initial study by the researchers, which relied on historical outbreak costs. The estimates from the tool are not a definitive accounting of what the outbreaks actually cost. But it illustrates the hefty bill tied to the spread of a disease that can be prevented through vaccination, and the potential economic consequences of falling childhood immunization rates. CNBC previously reported on how the return of measles in the U.S. exposes weaknesses in the nation’s public health system that raise concerns about the return of other eliminated diseases. “We’re starting to see a whole bunch of people who decided not to get vaccinated as kids or have never gotten exposed to measles because they benefited from herd immunity, who now make it into adulthood and risk potentially getting quite seriously ill and requiring very significant medical expenses, or dying at the hospital,” Bryan Patenaude, a health economist at the International Vaccine Access Center at the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health, who helped develop the tool, said in an interview. The costs of containing measles Measles outbreaks can trigger a costly public health response, even when many people who are exposed never become sick. When a case is identified, public health officials must investigate the infection, identify and notify people who may have been exposed, coordinate with hospitals and schools, and sometimes organize vaccination campaigns to prevent further spread. Those efforts can divert staff and resources from other public health priorities. watch now VIDEO3:0303:03 What the measles outbreak signals for the U.S. Health and Science Costs could differ heavily depending on the state. Johns Hopkins’ data includes national averages alongside state-specific inputs, including inpatient hospital costs and wages from government data or trusted health policy sources. In New York, for example, the average inpatient hospital stay is roughly $4,200 a day, while the minimum wage is $16 per hour. The tool estimates that the 48 cases reported by New York in 2025 carried an estimated $1.1 million in costs, including nearly $749,000 in public health responses, $333,000 in productivity losses and roughly $24,500 in direct medical expenses. Meanwhile, the 803 cases in Texas, the epicenter of the largest U.S. measles outbreak in 2025, carried estimated costs of nearly $13 million, according to the tool. That includes $9 million in public health costs, $4 million in productivity losses and around $288,000 in direct medical expenses. The tool builds on a study published by Patenaude and other researchers in August, which examined the costs of measles outbreaks in the U.S. The researchers reviewed published studies and government reports to compile historical estimates of medical expenses and public health response costs across 18 states. The paper estimated that outbreaks carried a historical average cost of nearly $59,000 per case, just from the public health response. The researchers also estimated how costs scale with outbreak size, separating fixed up-front response expenses from the additional costs associated with each new case. “A lot of this is incurred up front, and that’s because of the human labor that’s involved in contact tracing and all of the different actors that sort of need to be mobilized to deal with the fact that there’s an outbreak,” Patenaude told CNBC. “It’s a largely labor-driven cost.” That model estimated roughly $298,000 in fixed costs and about $16,300 for each additional case. But it relied on historical data, including a particularly large 2019 outbreak in New York City, where contact-tracing labor costs were high. The researchers also excluded productivity losses from that model because most of the historical studies they reviewed did not consistently account for them. The costs of lost productivity A medical assistant gives a measles, mumps and rubella, vaccination to a young person at the pediatric office of Dr. Gary M. Kramer in Coral Gables, Florida, Aug. 13, 2026. Joe Raedle | Getty Images The economic burden of measles extends beyond the costs of treating infected patients and organizing a public health response. People who are exposed to measles may have to quarantine, even if they never develop symptoms. Parents can also be forced to miss work to care for sick or exposed children, while workers who contract the disease may lose wages during their recovery. A 2025 study by Joey Mattingly, a pharmacist and health economist at the University of Utah, used published epidemiological and cost data to model the potential economic burden of a hypothetical 1,500-case outbreak. His analysis included direct medical expenses, public health spending and lost productivity, and examined how the estimates changed under different assumptions, including hospitalization rates. The study estimated that the hypothetical outbreak could cost about $90 million, including roughly $26 million in productivity losses. The economic ripple effects of a case Those indirect costs can add up quickly because measles is highly contagious and a single infected person can expose dozens of others. Those contacts may need to quarantine or miss work while health officials determine whether they have been infected, creating costs even when they never develop the disease. Mattingly estimated that each person who is quarantined could amount to roughly $300 in lost productivity alone. If a single measles case exposes 40 to 50 people who then need to quarantine, that could translate into an estimated $12,000 to $15,000 in lost productivity, even if none of those contacts become sick. MMR vaccine administer prep is pictured during a drive at the City of Lubbock Health Department in Texas, Feb. 27, 2025. Annie Rice | Reuters That ripple effect is one reason Mattingly argues that the economic costs of outbreaks can quickly outweigh the investment required to vaccinate people against measles. “Even if you don’t get the infection, the contact costs you,” he said. “And so it’s just showing that there’s this multiplication or exponential growth on the economic side that happens.” The financial burden can also fall unevenly across communities. While the costs of responding to an outbreak may be shared among local, state and federal agencies, Mattingly said the impact is often felt directly by local health departments. “It’s not like your health department is sitting around waiting for measles outbreaks,” he said. “They’re working, doing all these other things, and imagine having to just drop what you’re doing because there’s been a huge outbreak in the community.” Patenaude said the findings could help state and local health departments better anticipate the resources needed to contain outbreaks — particularly as measles cases continue to rise. “It’s not super expensive to treat and deal with a single case of measles compared to a single case of cancer,” he said. “But if you have these outbreaks popping up and you’re having to constantly divert resources that could be spent elsewhere by state and local health departments on measles, then you’re going to wind up spending a lot of money very quickly.” Choose CNBC as your preferred source on Google and never miss a moment from the most trusted name in business news. More than 5,100 measles cases have been reported in 2026, with 8 deaths. The economic cost of the 2025 outbreak was estimated at over $37 million, and the 2026 outbreak is estimated at nearly $83 million. The costs include public health response, lost productivity, and direct medical expenses.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

An Investigational Drug to Tackle Measles Outbreaks

News Brief
NanoViricides, Inc. is advancing its investigational drug NV-387 towards clinical trials for treating Measles. The USA has seen 5,164 confirmed Measles cases, with 4 deaths and 562 hospitalizations, mostly in unvaccinated children under 5. The D8 genotype of the Measles virus is dominant in 2026 US cases. NV-387 has shown strong in vivo activity against Measles in a humanized animal model and has been granted RPDD and PRV designations by the FDA, supporting its development for Measles treatment.
Original source text (English)
SHELTON, CT / ACCESS Newswire / November 10, 2026 / NanoViricides, Inc., a publicly traded company (NYSE American: NNVC ) (the “Company”), and a clinical stage, leading global pioneer in the development of broad-spectrum antivirals based on host-mimetic nanomedicine technology that viruses cannot escape, announces that it is advancing its Measles drug development program towards clinical trials. There have been three deaths in children due to Measles in the USA in the last ten days, one each in the states of New York, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin i . This condition should be unacceptable in the USA because there is already a drug against Measles that has shown effectiveness in a humanized animal model of Measles, that has gone through extensive studies including Phase I human safety study, and that is already in two Phase II clinical trials for other viral infections. This investigational drug is NV-387, a novel, revolutionary broad-spectrum, antiviral drug developed by the Company. This drug has completed a juvenile animal model study as well. NV-387 is not yet FDA-approved or commercially available for treating Measles or any other viral disease. However, we believe the drug NV-387 now has sufficient data available to enable use under the US FDA physician-initiated investigational new drug application mechanism that is designed for use of experimental drugs for specific severe cases of disease, such as Measles. The Company continues to develop this drug in the regulatory pathways. There were 5,164 confirmed Measles cases within the USA (excluding international visitors) as of November 5, 2026, reported in 47 jurisdictions, with 4% of these being breakthrough cases in vaccinated individuals, and remaining 96% in unvaccinated individuals. 562 (11%) of the cases were hospitalized, and 4 confirmed Measles-caused deaths were recorded. A few additional deaths in Measles cases that are under CDC review have also occurred. Majority of hospitalizations occurred in children under 5 years of age. Only 4 states, namely Arkansas, Hawaii, Mississippi, and Nevada have not reported a Measles case in 2026, according to the CDC ii . These data indicate that Measles in the USA is continuing to expand in 2026. At this point, loss of the Measles elimination status achieved by the USA in 2000 as the very first country to achieve this status would be merely symbolic, emphasizing that Measles is a globally endemic virus with a very high potential for creating a contagion. Vaccines, even though effective, are inadequate as the only response to such a disease as Measles, and drugs to treat Measles infections are sorely needed. Of note, the D8 genotype of the Measles virus dominates in 2026 US cases iii whereas the Measles vaccine is based on the A genotype. NV-387, the Company’s revolutionary clinical stage broad-spectrum antiviral drug is currently in a Phase II clinical trials as an oral treatment for the Ebola Bundibugyo virus epidemic in the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC). The same drug, NV-387, formulated as the same oral gummies formulation is also in a Phase II clinical trial for the treatment of Mpox (Monkeypox) in DRC in a different province. This very same drug has demonstrated remarkable antiviral activity against the Measles virus in a humanized hCD150+, IfnAR-/- mouse model of Measles virus infection and disease. The Company has also completed a repeat-dose juvenile mouse model toxicity study in which 4-day old mouse pups were dosed with repeated doses of NV-387 oral gummies formulation to evaluate the safety and tolerability of the drug and drug product in the context of the use of the drug in children. Under the conditions of this Juvenile Mouse Model study, the No Observed Adverse Effect Level (NOAEL) was determined to be 1,230 mg/kg per dose administered three times daily, or 3,690 mg/kg/day, or a total cumulative drug exposure of 44,280 mg/kg over the 12 day dosing period. This was actually the highest dose level tested. Therefore, a Maximum Tolerable Dose (MTD) could not be estimated iv . Further, there was no mortality, systemic toxicity, treatment-related clinical observations, adverse effects on body weight gain, alterations in cardiovascular parameters, changes in clinical pathology, or treatment-related gross or microscopic pathological findings observed in either sex. According to established guidelines for juvenile animal model data extrapolation, a drug evaluated for safety toxicology in 4 day mouse pups supports clinical pediatric study in infants as young as 1 month v , under an appropriate regulatory study. Cases of Measles have been rising globally for different reasons. The measles outbreak in Bangladesh has continued to proliferate, claiming 24,073 lab-confirmed cases with 105 fatalities in lab-confirmed cases from 15 March to 10 November, 2026. During this period, a total of 207,428 patients with suspected measles were hospitalized and 1,263 persons, mostly children, have died of suspected measles, while 1,318 new suspected measles cases with at least 6 more child deaths were reported in just the last 24 hours in Bangladesh vi . The measles outbreak in Bangladesh has continued despite aggressive vaccination campaign that was undertaken since April-May 2026. There is currently no approved drug for measles. Vaccine does not help a patient already infected with the measles virus. Vaccinating already measles-exposed persons to avoid disease spread is attempted yet measles has continued to spread in the USA. Thus an effective treatment for Measles is an unmet medical need. NV-387 is the only drug candidate to our knowledge that has demonstrated strong in vivo activity against lethal infection with the Measles virus in a humanized animal model study, supporting its clinical regulatory development for treatment of Measles. NanoViricides is currently developing a Phase II clinical trial protocol for evaluating NV-387 as a treatment for measles. A draft is being circulated. The Company intends to bring this protocol under the purview of the US FDA in an IND process. Therefore, this work is expected to take some time. NV-387 has been granted a “Rare Pediatric Disease Drug” (RPDD) Designation by the US FDA Office of Orphan Products Development (OOPD). This RPDD designation enables that a Priority Review Voucher (PRV) can be issued to the Company upon a successful Marketing Application (i.e. New Drug Application) for the use of NV-387 as a treatment for Measles, provided all of the required conditions are met. The Company can use the PRV, if granted, to reduce review times on another of its drug applications. A PRV can be sold to another Pharma Company for accelerating their drug program as well, which provides a high value to the purchaser. PRVs have been sold for as much as $200 Million vii . “The Rare Pediatric Disease Drug Designation and associated Priority Review Voucher that can be issued make a strong business case for development of NV-387 as a drug for the treatment of Measles,” said Anil R. Diwan, PhD., adding, “NV-387, as an effective drug would be an important tool to fight Measles resurgence in the USA and worldwide, when approved.” FDA has granted both ODD and RPDD for NV-387 as a Treatment of Measles. In addition to the PRV eligibility provided by the RPDD for NV-387 as a treatment of Measles, the Orphan Drug Designation qualifies NanoViricides for incentives including: Tax credits for qualified clinical trials; Exemption from certain user fees; Potential seven years of market exclusivity after approval; according to the US FDA viii . Measles continues to be a rare disease in the USA, with annual incidence rates well below 200,000 cases. Measles primarily affects children. These facts have qualified NV-387 for Measles Treatment for an Orphan Drug Designation, and also for a Rare Pediatric Disease Drug Designation. In a rare disease scenario, commercial revenue from the drug may be slow and small. The PRV is designed to incentivize drug development for rare pediatric diseases by providing additional business value. Measles cases are rising across the Western world including several European countries and the UK, as well as the USA and Canada. Additionally Mexico and several other Central and South American countries have also been suffering from rising Measles outbreaks. Measles is endemic globally; many countries that had achieved an elimination status for Measles have by now lost this status, due to severe outbreaks over the last three years ix . Measles is a highly contagious disease, and spreads via virus particles in bodily fluids upon close contact. A population vaccination rate of at least 95% is required to provide “herd immunity” and block community spread. Such a high rate is difficult to maintain for several reasons. Firstly, vaccine hesitancy is rising globally. Secondly, persons with co-morbidities such as immune-compromise status, diabetes, obesity, etc. do not respond fully to any vaccination. Even if vaccinated, such a person can still get Measles, suffering from possibly a mild disease, but will still be contagious and spread Measles. Additionally, the Measles virus continues to change, with the B and D genotypes in circulation currently, while the vaccines in use are still the ones developed for the A genotype in the 1960’s. Further, very high rates of infections in vaccinated subjects (called “breakthrough cases”), from 14% to 57%, have occurred in some outbreaks x suggesting that resistant virus strains may have been involved. Therefore, an effective treatment for Measles is an unmet medical need, that is expected to become even more important in the near future than it is today. Only an effective treatment can help the patient and can avoid the potential severe disease scenarios such as encephalitis, neurological disabilities, and potential fatalities as well as immune amnesia that can result from severe disease. Quicker recovery of a patient means less chances for spread and thus better ability to control an outbreak. This would require an effective treatment. NV-387 is an extremely broad-spectrum antiviral drug that has demonstrated strong effectiveness in relevant animal models of multiple human viral infections. These include RSV, COVID, Influenza, Mpox, Smallpox, and Measles. ABOUT NANOVIRICIDES NanoViricides, Inc. (the “Company”) ( www.nanoviricides.com ) is a publicly traded (NYSE-American, stock symbol NNVC) clinical stage company that is creating special purpose nanomaterials for antiviral therapy. The Company’s novel nanoviricide TM class of drug candidates and the nanoviricide TM technology are based on intellectual property, technology and proprietary know-how of TheraCour Pharma, Inc. The Company has a Memorandum of Understanding with TheraCour for the development of drugs based on these technologies for all antiviral infections. The MoU does not include cancer and similar diseases that may have viral origin but require different kinds of treatments. The Company has obtained broad, exclusive, sub-licensable, field licenses to drugs developed in several licensed fields from TheraCour Pharma, Inc. The Company’s business model is based on licensing technology from TheraCour Pharma Inc. for specific application verticals of specific viruses, as established at its foundation in 2005. Our lead drug candidate is NV-387, a broad-spectrum antiviral drug that we plan to develop as a treatment of RSV, COVID, Long COVID, Influenza, and other respiratory viral infections, as well as MPOX/Smallpox infections. Our other advanced drug candidate is NV-HHV-1 for the treatment of Shingles. The Company cannot project an exact date for filing an IND for any of its drugs because of dependence on a number of external collaborators and consultants. The Company is currently focused on advancing NV-387 into Phase II human clinical trials. The Company is also developing drugs against a number of viral diseases including oral and genital Herpes, viral diseases of the eye including EKC and herpes keratitis, H1N1 swine flu, H5N1 bird flu, seasonal Influenza, HIV, Hepatitis C, Rabies, Dengue fever, and Ebola virus, among others. NanoViricides’ platform technology and programs are based on the TheraCour ® nanomedicine technology of TheraCour, which TheraCour licenses from AllExcel. NanoViricides holds a worldwide exclusive perpetual license to this technology for several drugs with specific targeting mechanisms in perpetuity for the treatment of the following human viral diseases: Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV/AIDS), Hepatitis B Virus (HBV), Hepatitis C Virus (HCV), Rabies, Herpes Simplex Virus (HSV-1 and HSV-2), Varicella-Zoster Virus (VZV), Influenza and Asian Bird Flu Virus, Dengue viruses, Japanese Encephalitis virus, West Nile Virus, Ebola/Marburg viruses, and certain Coronaviruses. The Company intends to obtain a license for RSV, Poxviruses, and/or Enteroviruses if the initial research is successful. As is customary, the Company must state the risk factor that the path to typical drug development of any pharmaceutical product is extremely lengthy and requires substantial capital. As with any drug development efforts by any company, there can be no assurance at this time that any of the Company’s pharmaceutical candidates would show sufficient effectiveness and safety for human clinical development. Further, there can be no assurance at this time that successful results against coronavirus in our lab will lead to successful clinical trials or a successful pharmaceutical product. How word of mouth is helping an Amish community's fight against measles - NPR

News Brief
In Pennsylvania, health officials are using a quiet, word-of-mouth campaign to vaccinate Amish families against measles, which has caused 6 deaths and over 1,500 cases. The Amish community, with low vaccination rates, is particularly vulnerable to the highly contagious disease. Health workers like Dale Weiser are visiting homes to address vaccine misinformation and build trust, aiming to increase vaccination rates and protect the community.
