Living Better Here's what to know if you're unsure about your measles immunity status Weiser is the clinical services supervisor for the Chester County Health Department in south-central Pennsylvania. Since August, she's been driving out to Amish homes to vaccinate people against measles. She says it usually starts with a phone call. "I was at a house, like, at 4:30 in the afternoon, and I had a phone call at 8 the next morning saying, 'hey, I heard you were down the street. Can you come do that at my house?'" she says of a typical request she gets from Amish families. So far, she's administered more than 400 doses of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine in Amish homes. The house calls are part of the state of Pennsylvania's strategy to wage war on measles by flipping the script on traditional public health messaging. Instead of advertising vaccinations loudly, health officials have launched a quiet word-of-mouth campaign designed to win the trust of the state's Amish community, which has been particularly hard hit by the country's worst measles outbreak in 35 years. The state has confirmed six measles-related deaths and more than 1,500 cases so far — though the true count is likely much higher, since many people who get sick don't seek medical care. Lower vaccination rates Surveys suggest only about a third of Amish families in the region vaccinate their children. That's left the Amish community especially vulnerable to measles, one of the most contagious diseases. There's no religious edict against vaccination among the Amish. It's very much seen as a family and individual choice, says Weiser. Often, she says, these decisions are driven by practical and cultural factors. Animals graze at a farm in Bird-in-Hand, an agricultural community in Lancaster County, Pa., with a large Amish and Mennonite population, on Oct. 29, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption toggle caption i Daniel Vasta for NPR "They don't have health insurance. They're not interacting with the mainstream medical society. And measles wasn't a problem for 30 years, so there was no real risk to not being vaccinated, at least for measles," Weiser says. But as measles cases have piled up in Pennsylvania, Weiser says many Amish families have changed their minds about getting the MMR vaccine. And when they do, she hops in her car and brings the shots to them. "When I arrive at their home, there's usually a small child that will run from the back of the house to the barn to tell the men that there's somebody strange in the driveway, or that the nurse is here," she says. A patient, personalized approach to outreach Some days, Weiser will vaccinate a single family in their kitchen. Other times, she'll find dozens of people gathered to get the shot. She's also fielded a lot of questions. Some, like what's in the vaccine and potential side effects, are things anyone would ask, she says. "But I was caught off guard when I was asked if there were tracking chips in the vaccine, and I was like, whoa. Flashback to COVID. Where did you hear that?" she recalls. Want the latest stories on the science of healthy living? Subscribe to NPR's Health newsletter. As with the general population, vaccine misinformation has made its way to the Amish community. And when she encounters it, Weiser takes the time to explain why the rumors are false. Chester County Public Health Director Jeanne Franklin says this kind of patient, personalized approach has helped build trust among the local Amish – who, along with conservative Mennonites, are known as the Plain community because of their simple way of life. A placard reads "work with me people" in the office of Jeanne Franklin, the public health director at the Chester County Health Department, in West Chester, Pa., on Oct. 28, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption toggle caption i Daniel Vasta for NPR "That's why we said it has to be boots on the ground in the communities. It's not just because they were a Plain community, it was because we needed to be seen as caring people," Franklin says. Instead of relying on traditional top-down, rigid public health messaging, her staff has spent a lot of time quietly visiting Amish church leaders and businesses, introducing themselves and asking community leaders if they would hand out flyers advertising vaccine clinics. "One of them said, 'Come on back with a clinic' at his restaurant. And he helped spread the word," Franklin says. Vaccine questions and misinformation When I stopped by the restaurant, Honey Brook Barbecue in Honey Brook, Pa., an area with a sizable Amish community, I found the manager grilling chicken in an outdoor tent. Like many Amish people NPR talked to, he spoke on the condition of anonymity in keeping with his community's tradition of not drawing attention to oneself. A decorative sign on display near a hand washing station at Honey Brook Barbecue in Honey Brook, Pa., on Oct. 29, 2026. In August, the Amish-owned restaurant quietly hosted a vaccine clinic for the local community. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption toggle caption i Daniel Vasta for NPR The manager says 24 people showed up to get vaccinated at his restaurant back in August, all drawn by word of mouth. He says he decided to host the clinic for two reasons: to protect the general public and to protect his own community. He said he didn't want people blaming the Amish for spreading measles. But he says other people in the Amish community who don't regularly interact with the public aren't getting vaccinated. For some, it's because of cultural reasons, but he says others are concerned about what they've heard is in the MMR vaccine. Short Wave Vaccinated or not, we're on the brink of a measles epidemic "In my community, a lot of it has to do with what people think is in it," the manager says of the MMR vaccine. One concern he's heard repeatedly is that the vaccine contains parts of aborted fetuses. That is a myth often repeated by anti-vaccine forces, and it is one heard in conversations with Amish people in Lancaster County, home to around 45,000 Amish people. It's also the county with a third of the state's measles cases — more than 500 confirmed cases. At the Amish-owned Gordonville Book Store, where a buggy is parked outside, I meet a shopper named Lydia Esh. She says she, her husband and six adult children had all previously been vaccinated against measles. But none of her 13 grandchildren have gotten the shot, because their parents had heard that the MMR vaccine had changed and now contained aborted fetus parts — which is false. A buggy parked in the driveway at Gordonville Book Store in Gordonville, Pa., on Oct. 29, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption toggle caption i Daniel Vasta for NPR There is some evidence that anti-vaccine groups have been targeting the Amish in recent years, says Steve Nolt, a professor at Elizabethtown College who has written numerous books about Amish history and culture. For example, he points to an advertisement questioning the safety of childhood vaccines published in The Pennsylvania Busy Beaver, an advertising publication with a large Amish readership. But in conversations around Lancaster, I also find evidence that public health outreach efforts are working. One member of the Plain community, a visibly pregnant woman outside an Amish-owned hardware store, says her husband and two children recently got vaccinated to protect her and her unborn child. She asked NPR not to use her name, as did an Amish man working at a produce stall in an Amish market. He says he recently got vaccinated because "measles is scary." Linda Beth Fisher, an 18-year-old Amish woman behind the counter at the Gordonville Book Store, says she is concerned that several Amish people in her area have died in the measles outbreak, but she's not sure what to believe about the MMR vaccine. Information packets are set out for patients before a Pennsylvania Department of Health MMR vaccine pop-up clinic hosted at Christiana Fire Company in Christiana, Pa., on Oct. 29, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption toggle caption i Daniel Vasta for NPR "Personally, I'm kind of leery of it. I'm not sure why," Fisher says. "I guess I've just kind of heard that they're not good, but I've also heard that they are good. So I just feel like it's two sides of the story. Would you encourage people to get it?" "They almost always come back" These types of concerns often surface at pop-up vaccine clinics like one recently hosted at the Christiana Fire Company in Lancaster County. The Pennsylvania Department of Health hosts these events in Lancaster three or four times a week — all advertised by word of mouth, says Dr. Jennifer Wallace. As medical director for the department's Bureau of Community Health Systems, she oversees pop-up clinics and outreach to vulnerable communities across Pennsylvania. Bubble wands are set out for children before a Pennsylvania Department of Health MMR vaccine pop-up clinic hosted at Christiana Fire Company in Christiana, Pa., on Oct. 29, 2026. Daniel Vasta for NPR hide caption toggle caption i Daniel Vasta for NPR Wallace says when members of the Plain community come with questions, "We're here to give them that information and then let them ponder it. They can ask any questions they want to and then let them either get vaccinated if they choose or take that information home. Think about it. Talk with their bishops. Talk with their community. They almost always come back." Wallace says many who end up coming are families with multiple children. Tables set up around the firehouse are strewn with crayons, coloring books and bubble wands to keep young ones entertained while parents fill out paperwork. Health As diseases loom, CDC's response to measles deaths stirs fears about agency's reliability "And then you see the kids get vaccinated and you can almost see the mother's shoulders relax over time as each child gets that shot," she says. "We've had some mothers cry afterwards because they're just so relieved." The state health department says 30 people attended the Christiana firehouse clinic, 24 of whom got vaccinated. That included a father and his three sons who arrived for their second doses. NPR does not offer or accept money for coverage or interviews. 威斯康星州报告第 8 例与 麻疹 相关的死亡:卫生官员 - ABC7 Bay Area

8th measles-related death reported in Wisconsin: Health officials - ABC7 Bay Area
新闻简报据威斯康星州卫生部报告,威斯康星州一名婴儿死于 麻疹,这是该州自 1990 年以来首次出现此类死亡。这一事件使美国与 麻疹 相关的死亡总数达到 8 例,其中宾夕法尼亚州 6 例,纽约州 1 例,威斯康星州 1 例。威斯康星州今年记录了 431 例 麻疹 病例,是 35 年来最高的,其中 9 人需要住院治疗。CDC 强调,今年美国超过 96% 的 麻疹 病例涉及未接种疫苗或未知 疫苗接种 状态的个人。卫生官员强调了 疫苗接种 的重要性,并指出两剂 MMR 疫苗 预防 麻疹 的有效率为 97%。高风险地区 6 至 11 个月大的婴儿可能会提前接种第三剂。CDC 正在监测 暴发 并发布了预防和 疫苗接种 指南。

时间
2026 年 11 月 10 日星期四,威斯康星州报道了第 8 起与 麻疹 相关的死亡事件。新闻报道的发布日期为 2026 年 11 月 10 日。

经州卫生部门证实,威斯康星州一名婴儿死于 麻疹。这是该州自 1990 年以来首例 麻疹 死亡事件。

卫生官员已确认死亡并报告了 暴发 的详细信息。CDC 建议接种两剂 MMR 疫苗,一剂有效率 93%,两剂有效率 97%。高风险地区 6 至 11 个月大的婴儿可能会提前接种第三剂。CDC 正在监测 暴发 并已发布预防指南。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

8th measles-related death reported in Wisconsin: Health officials - ABC7 Bay Area

News Brief
An infant in Wisconsin has died from measles, marking the first such death in the state since 1990, as reported by the Wisconsin Department of Health. This incident brings the total number of measles-related deaths in the U.S. to eight, with six in Pennsylvania, one in New York, and one in Wisconsin. Wisconsin has recorded 431 measles cases this year, the highest in over 35 years, with nine individuals requiring hospitalization. The CDC highlights that over 96% of measles cases in the U.S. this year involve unvaccinated or unknown vaccination status individuals. Health officials emphasize the importance of vaccination, noting that two doses of the MMR vaccine are 97% effective in preventing measles. Infants aged 6 to 11 months in high-risk areas may receive an early third dose. The CDC is monitoring the outbreak and has issued guidance on prevention and vaccination.
Original source text (English)
BreakingFAA ground stop live updates: Nationwide ground stop lifted 24/7 LiveSan FranciscoEast BaySouth BayPeninsulaNorth Bay 58° Measles 8th measles-related death reported in Wisconsin: Health officials It's the first measles death in the state since 1990. ByYouri Benadjaoud Tuesday, November 10, 2026 12:37PM Watch ABC News live news stream and get 24/7 latest, breaking news coverage, and live video. An infant in Wisconsin has died from measles, the state health department said Thursday. It's the first death in the state since 1990. This brings the total number of measles deaths reported by state health departments across the country to eight: six in Pennsylvania, one in New York and one in Wisconsin. Wisconsin has reported 431 measles cases so far this year, the highest number in the state in more than 35 years. Nine people have required hospitalization, according to health officials. "It is with a heavy heart we are reporting the first death of an infant connected with a measles outbreak in Wisconsin in many years. My heart is broken for the family," Wisconsin Department of Health Secretary Kirsten Johnson said in a press release. "Any death of a child is a tragedy and even more so when it is related to a vaccine-preventable disease. Measles can cause serious illness and death, and it is important for all Wisconsinites to evaluate their risk and take steps to protect themselves and their family." At least 96% of measles cases in the U.S. this year have been among people who are unvaccinated or whose vaccination status is unknown. Early symptoms include cough, runny nose, fever as high as 104 degrees Fahrenheit and red, watery eyes, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC). Top Stories FAA ground stop live updates: Duffy blames 'overloaded' routers; thousands of flights canceled 5 minutes ago San Francisco Bay Area Weather: Level 2 storm brings heavy rain, winds and dangerous high surf to region 1 hour ago Here's how the FAA's national ground stop is affecting Bay Area travel 2 hours ago Two to three days after symptoms start, patients may develop Koplik spots, which are tiny white spots that may appear inside the mouth on the inner cheek. Three to five days after symptoms start, patients may experience a rash, first appearing on the face at the hairline before moving downwards to the neck, chest, stomach, arms, legs and feet. Fever may spike even higher than 104 F when the rash appears, the CDC says. Nationally, more than 4,800 measles cases have been confirmed this year in the U.S., with infections reported in almost every state, according to the CDC. It's the highest number of illnesses from measles since 1991. The CDC currently recommends that people receive two doses of the MMR vaccine, the first at ages 12 to 15 months and the second between 4 and 6 years old. One dose is 93% effective in preventing measles infection, and two doses are 97% effective, the CDC says. Infants aged 6 to 11 months living in or traveling to high-risk outbreak areas may receive an early third dose of the MMR vaccine. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Second Wisconsin child dies of measles - WPR

News Brief
A second child in Grant County, Wisconsin, has died from measles, marking the second death in the state since 1990. The outbreak has seen 651 cases across 22 counties, the highest number in over 35 years. Health officials recommend early measles vaccination for infants, citing low vaccination rates as a contributing factor to the spread of the virus.
Original source text (English)
Skip to content Skip to Audio Player Toggle Site Mobile Navigation Home News Shows Culture Music ABOUT Who We Are Events Careers / Volunteer LISTEN Stations & Schedules Music Playlists Ways to Listen SUPPORT Ways to Give Membership FAQ Funding Information CONTACT Get in Touch Help / FAQ Newsletters POLICIES Ethical Guidelines Policies and Reports Privacy Policy Public Inspection Files Public File Assistance News Shows Culture Music Wisconsin Public Radio Homepage Donate Search wpr.org Children’s Health, Health, News Second Wisconsin child dies of measles DHS reported another pediatric death in Grant County as measles continues to spread in southwest outbreak By Hope Kirwan November 13, 2026 Updated November 13, 2026 at 5:01 pm A measles sign is seen at the Texas Tech University Health Sciences Center, Feb. 25, 2025, in Lubbock, Texas. AP Photo/Julio Cortez, file A second child in Grant County has died of measles, according to state health officials. The Wisconsin Department of Health Services announced the pediatric measles-associated death on Friday. No further details were released to protect the privacy of the child and their family. The news comes one week after DHS announced an infant had died of the virus, marking the first measles death in the state since 1990. Understanding Wisconsin, Together. WPR’s “Wisconsin Today” newsletter keeps you connected to the state you love without feeling overwhelmed. No paywall. No agenda. No corporate filter. Instagram This field is for validation purposes and should be left unchanged. Email Measles is one of the most contagious viruses, and it can cause life-threatening complications including pneumonia and swelling of the brain. Children younger than age 5 are among those who are most at risk of serious illness, as well as people who are pregnant or who have weakened immune systems. Wisconsin health officials advise early measles vaccination for some infants amid outbreak Doctors recommend children receive the first dose of the two-shot measles, mumps and rubella, or MMR, vaccine after their first birthday. But DHS announced last week that it recommends children as young as 6 months who are at increased risk of exposure to measles get the shot. The two-dose course of the MMR vaccine is 97 percent effective at preventing measles and provides lifelong protection. As of Friday, there have been 651 measles cases reported across 22 Wisconsin counties this year. It’s the highest number of cases in the state in more than 35 years, according to DHS, and likely does not reflect the full extent of spread due to underreporting. Speaking to PBS Wisconsin’s Here & Now, Dr. Ryan Westergaard, chief medical officer and state epidemiologist for communicable diseases at DHS, said it’s safe to assume the state has “well over 1,000 cases.” “When we investigate cases in the community, we hear about families saying that there are other families that we don’t know about,” he said. “We know that with all of our conditions that are reportable to public health, they don’t all get reported. Some people don’t get tested, some people don’t come to medical attention.” Westergaard pointed out that in the decade before the COVID-19 pandemic, Wisconsin saw between zero and two cases of measles most years. But he said the unchecked spread of the virus this year is not surprising given the state’s low childhood vaccination rates. “This is really something that we haven’t seen in our careers, and we fear we’re not quite through it,” he said. “It’s likely to continue to get worse.” Wisconsin has the fourth-highest number of measles cases in the U.S. this year, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. Pennsylvania, which has the most measles cases for the year, reported the state’s seventh-measles related death on Friday. New York health officials reported Monday that a child had died of the virus, marking that state’s first measles death in more than 30 years. Deputy Director Leaves Peptide Injections Hepatitis B Vaccine Advertisement SKIP ADVERTISEMENT Supported by SKIP ADVERTISEMENT When Measles Came to an Amish Community, Many Suffered and One Died In Lewis County, N.Y., the highly contagious virus has swept through Amish homes. Public health experts fear there’s no end in sight. Listen · 9:35 min Share full article A towering pile of logs attested to work unfinished amid a measles outbreak in Lewis County, N.Y.Credit...David Andreatta By Joseph Goldstein and David Andreatta Joseph Goldstein reported from New York City, and David Andreatta from Lewis County, N.Y., where an infant died from measles. Nov. 11, 2026 On a recent Saturday morning in New York’s North Country, there were signs that work had fallen behind. Standing on his porch in suspenders and shirtsleeves, 32-year-old David Stoltzfus pointed at a heap of logs, some thicker than telephone poles and stacked two stories high. He needed to saw them into lumber. But Mr. Stoltzfus was still playing catch-up after falling ill. “You see the big pile of wood that all got piled up?” he asked, pointing to hundreds of logs. “That’s because of the measles.” Subscribe to The Times to read as many articles as you like. Joseph Goldstein covers health care in New York for The Times, following years of criminal justice and police reporting. A version of this article appears in print on Nov. 13, 2026, Section A, Page 10 of the New York edition with the headline: Measles Engulfs an Amish Enclave in New York. Location and population
Lewis County, New York, USA; Amish community.
Event
A measles outbreak has swept through the Amish community in Lewis County, New York, with many suffering and one death reported.
Scale, impact and risk
One death and many cases reported; the outbreak is highly contagious and public health experts fear no end in sight.
Response, status and uncertainty
The investigation is ongoing; measures such as isolation and contact tracing are being implemented.

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Daviess Co., Ind. Health Department confirms positive measles case - WFIE | 14 News

News Brief
The Daviess County Health Department in Indiana, United States, has confirmed a positive measles case. The individual is receiving medical attention, and health officials recommend vaccination as the best protection against measles. No deaths or exposures have been reported.

大砍防疫人力、動搖疫苗政策嘗苦果 美國恐失「麻疹消除國」地位

News article (Chinese original; verified English title unavailable)

很難想像,美國這樣的全球頭等大國,可能即將失去維持26年的「麻疹消除國」地位。泛美衛生組織(PAHO)本週將召開專家會議,審查美國是否已經出現連續12個月的麻疹病毒持續傳播。如果判定符合條件,美國將失去2000年以來取得的「麻疹消除國」資格。

大砍防疫人力、動搖疫苗政策嘗苦果 美國恐失「麻疹消除國」地位
